(A,B)\square(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})=(A\otimes A^{\prime},B\diamond B^{\prime}),

where

(8.7)

(B\diamond B^{\prime})_{n}=\sum_{k+l=n}\>\sum_{(\mu,\nu)\in{\sf Sh}(k,l)}s_{\nu}(B_{k})\>\bar{\otimes}\>s_{\mu}(B^{\prime}_{l}).

Note that this formula is similar to the formula of the Eilenberg-Zilber map. In order to prove that
B\diamond B^{\prime}
is a path submodule of
A\otimes A^{\prime},
we need a lemma.

Lemma 8.1
.

The graded module
B\diamond B^{\prime}
can be defined as

(8.8)

(B\diamond B^{\prime})_{n}=\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n)}f^{*}(B_{|f|})\>\bar{\otimes}\>g^{*}(B^{\prime}_{|g|}).

and as

(8.9)

(B\diamond B^{\prime})_{n}=\sum_{(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}_{\square}(n)}\sigma^{*}(B_{|\sigma|})\>\bar{\otimes}\>\tau^{*}(B^{\prime}_{|\tau|}).

Proof.

Take
(f,g)\in\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n;k,l)
and consider its standard decomposition
(f,g)=(\alpha\sigma s^{\nu}\sigma,\beta s^{\mu}),
where
(\mu,\nu)\in{\sf Sh}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
(see (
6.9
)). Then
f^{*}(B_{|f|})\>\bar{\otimes}\>g^{*}(B^{\prime}_{|g|})\subseteq s_{\nu}(B_{k^{\prime}})\>\bar{\otimes}\>s_{\mu}(B_{l^{\prime}}).

∎

Corollary 8.2
.

B\diamond B^{\prime}
is a path submodule of
A\otimes A^{\prime}.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
8.1
and the fact that
\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}
is natural by
[n],[k],[l]\in\Pi.

∎

Lemma 8.3
.

The following inclusion holds.

(8.10)

(B\diamond B^{\prime})_{n}\subseteq\bigoplus_{(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}_{\square}(n)}\sigma^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{|\sigma|}A)\otimes\tau^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{|\tau|}A^{\prime}).

In other words, for any
x\in(B\diamond B^{\prime})_{n}
and any
(\sigma,\tau)\in{\sf PS}(n)\setminus{\sf PS}_{\square}(n)

we have
x_{\sigma,\tau}=0.

Proof.

Note that
B_{k}\subseteq A_{k}=\bigoplus_{\psi:[k]\twoheadrightarrow[k^{\prime}]}\psi^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{k^{\prime}}A).
Then for any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}_{\square}(n;k,l)
we have that
\sigma^{*}(B_{k})\otimes\tau^{*}(B^{\prime}_{l})
is included into the sum of modules

(\psi\sigma)^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{k}A)\otimes(\phi\tau)^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{l}A^{\prime})
over all pairs of
surjections
\psi:[k]\twoheadrightarrow[k^{\prime}]
and
\phi:[l]\twoheadrightarrow[l^{\prime}].

Since
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}_{\square}(n;k,l),
we have
(\psi\sigma,\phi\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}_{\square}(n;k^{\prime},l^{\prime}).

Then the assertion follows from Lemma
8.1
.
∎

For two complexes with graded submodules we set

(C,D)\otimes(C^{\prime},D^{\prime})=(C\otimes C^{\prime},D\bar{\otimes}D^{\prime}).

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\mathpzc{P}=(A,B)
and
\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}=(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
be two path pairs. Then the Eilenberg-Zilber and Alexander-Whitney maps induce morphisms of complexes with graded submodules

(8.11)

\varepsilon:\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}\otimes\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathsf{N}(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}),

(8.12)

\alpha:\mathsf{N}(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}\otimes\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}.

Proof.

Since, for any shuffle
(\mu,\nu)
we have
(s^{\mu},s^{\nu})\in\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n),
we obtain
\mathcal{E}(B\bar{\otimes}B^{\prime})\subseteq B\diamond B^{\prime}.

Using that the diagram (
7.20
) is commutative, we get

\varepsilon(\overline{B}\bar{\otimes}\overline{B}^{\prime})\subseteq\overline{B\diamond B^{\prime}}.
Then the morphism
\varepsilon:\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}\otimes\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}\to\mathsf{N}(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime})
is well defined. Since
B
and
B^{\prime}
are closed with respect to exterior faces, we obtain
\mathcal{A}(B\diamond B^{\prime})\subseteq B\bar{\otimes}B^{\prime}.
Using that the diagram (
7.20
) is commutative, we get
\alpha(\overline{B\diamond B^{\prime}})\subseteq\overline{B}\bar{\otimes}\overline{B}^{\prime}.
Then the map
\alpha:\mathsf{N}(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}\otimes\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}
is well defined.
∎

8.4.
Homotopy invariance

We denote by
\Delta^{n}
the standard
n
-simplex and by
d^{0},d^{1}:\Delta^{0}\to\Delta^{1}
the two embeddings of
0
-simplex to the
1
-simplex. Consider the path pair of modules given by

(8.13)

I^{\sf p}=(\mathbb{K}[\Delta^{1}],\mathbb{K}[\Delta^{1}]),\hskip 28.45274pt{\sf pt}^{\sf p}=(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{K})

and two morphisms between them induces by
d^{0},d^{1}

(8.14)

i_{0},i_{1}:{\sf pt}^{\sf p}\longrightarrow I^{\sf p}.

Note that
{\sf pt}^{\sf p}\square\mathpzc{P}\cong\mathpzc{P}.
Then we obtain a weak cylinder functor

(8.15)

{\sf cyl}(\mathpzc{P})=\mathpzc{P}\square I^{\sf p}

and define homotopic morphisms of path pairs via this weak cylinder functor.

Theorem 8.5
.

Any homotopic morphisms of path pairs
f\sim g:\mathpzc{P}\to\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}
induce homotopic maps

(8.16)

\Omega f\sim\Omega g:\Omega\mathpzc{P}\longrightarrow\Omega\mathpzc{P}^{\prime},\hskip 28.45274pt\Psi f\sim\Psi g:\Psi\mathpzc{P}\longrightarrow\Psi\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}.

Proof.

By (
3.18
) we have

{\sf cyl}(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P})\cong(\mathsf{N}A\otimes I^{\sf c},\overline{B}\otimes I^{\sf g}).
On the other hand

I^{\sf c}=\mathsf{N}(\mathbb{K}[\Delta^{1}]).
Therefore,
\mathsf{N}A\otimes I^{\sf c}=\mathsf{N}A\otimes\mathsf{N}(\mathbb{K}[\Delta^{1}])
and
{\sf cyl}(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P})=\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}\otimes\mathsf{N}I^{\sf p}.

Then the we have the Eilenberg-Zilber map (Lemma
8.4
)

(8.17)

\varepsilon:{\sf Cyl}(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P})\to\mathsf{N}({\sf cyl}(\mathpzc{P})).

We claim that the triangle

(8.18)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf cyl}(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}({\sf cyl}(\mathpzc{P}))}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{n}_{\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}(i^{n}{P})}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

is commutative for
n=0,1
. Indeed, for any
a\in(\mathsf{N}A)_{m}
we have
i_{n}(a)=(-1)^{n}a\otimes d^{n},
where
d^{0},d^{1}
are corresponding elements from
(\Delta^{1})_{0}.
There is only one
(m,0)
-shuffle, and hence,
\varepsilon(i^{n}_{\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{P}}(a))=(-1)^{n}a\otimes d^{n}=\mathsf{N}(i^{n}{P})(a).
So the triangle is commutative. The assertion follows from Proposition
2.1
.
∎

8.5.
Eilenberg-Zilber theorem for
\Omega

Theorem 8.6

(cf.
[
13
, Th.7.6]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a principal ideal domain and let
\mathpzc{P}=(A,B)
and
\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}=(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
be two path pairs of modules such that:

•

A_{n}
and
A_{n}^{\prime}
are free modules for any
n\geq 0;

•

\overline{B}_{n}
and
\overline{B}^{\prime}_{n}
are direct summands of
\mathsf{N}_{n}B
and
\mathsf{N}_{n}B^{\prime}
respectively.

Then the Eilenberg-Zilber and Alexander-Whitney maps (
7.18
) induce mutually inverse isomorphism of complexes

(8.19)

\Omega\mathpzc{P}\otimes\Omega\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}\cong\Omega(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}).

Moreover, there is a short exact sequence

(8.20)

0\to\bigoplus_{i+j=n}H_{i}\mathpzc{P}\otimes H_{j}\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}\to H_{n}(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime})\to\bigoplus_{i+j=n-1}{\sf Tor}^{\mathbb{K}}_{1}(H_{i}\mathpzc{P},H_{j}\mathpzc{P}^{\prime})\to 0.

Remark 8.7
.

Note that in the ordinary Eilenberg-Zilber theorem for simplicial modules, the complexes
\mathsf{N}A\otimes\mathsf{N}A^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{N}(A\otimes A^{\prime})
are generally not isomorphic, the first one is just a homotopy retract in the second one. But in Theorem
8.6
, following
[
13
, Th.7.6]
, we obtain a stronger result, an isomorphism of complexes.

Since
\mathbb{K}
is a principal ideal domain and the modules
A_{n},A^{\prime}_{n}
are free, then their submodules
\mathsf{N}_{n}A,\mathsf{N}_{n}A^{\prime},\Omega_{n}\mathpzc{P},\Omega_{n}\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}
are also free, and the map
\Omega\mathpzc{P}\otimes\Omega\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}\to\mathsf{N}A\otimes\mathsf{N}A^{\prime}
is injective. So we can identify
\Omega\mathpzc{P}\otimes\Omega\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}
with a submodule of
\mathsf{N}A\otimes\mathsf{N}A^{\prime}.
By the same reason we identify
B_{k}\otimes B^{\prime}_{l}
with the submodule of
A_{k}\otimes A^{\prime}_{l}
and identify
\bar{B}_{k}\otimes\bar{B}^{\prime}_{l}
with the submodule of
\mathsf{N}_{k}A\otimes\mathsf{N}_{l}A^{\prime}.
In order to prove Theorem
8.6
we need two lemmas.

Lemma 8.8
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
8.6
the Eilenberg-Zilber and Alexander-Whitney maps can be restricted to the maps of subcomplexes

(8.21)

\varepsilon^{\prime}:\Omega\mathpzc{P}\otimes\Omega\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}\rightleftarrows\Omega(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}):\alpha^{\prime}

such that
\alpha^{\prime}\varepsilon^{\prime}={\sf id}.

More precisely, there are inclusions

(8.22)

\varepsilon(\Omega\mathpzc{P}\otimes\Omega\mathpzc{P}^{\prime})\subseteq\Omega(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}),\hskip 28.45274pt\alpha(\Omega(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}))\subseteq\Omega\mathpzc{P}\otimes\Omega\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}.

Proof.

Lemma
8.4
implies that these maps can be restricted to the maps

(8.23)

\omega(\mathsf{N}A\otimes\mathsf{N}A^{\prime},\overline{B}\otimes\overline{B}^{\prime})\leftrightarrows\Omega(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}).

The assertion follows from Proposition
3.11
.
∎

Lemma 8.9

(cf.
[
13
, Prop.7.12]
)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
8.6
there is an inclusion

(8.24)

\Omega(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{Im}(\varepsilon:\mathsf{N}A\otimes\mathsf{N}A^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathsf{N}(A\otimes A^{\prime})).

Proof.

By Lemma
7.5
we need to prove that for any
x\in\Omega(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime})
and any
0\leq k,l\leq n
such that
k+l=n
we have

(8.25)

{\sf sgn}(\mu,\nu)x_{s^{\nu},s^{\mu}}={\sf sgn}(\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})x_{s^{\nu^{\prime}},s^{\mu^{\prime}}}

and
x_{\sigma,\tau}=0
for any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}^{\sf N}(n)\setminus\mathsf{PS}^{\sf N}_{\square}(n).

Consider a preimage
\tilde{x}\in B\diamond B^{\prime}
of
x
i.e. an element such that
\rho(\tilde{x})=x.
Then by Lemma
8.3
we have
\tilde{x}_{\sigma,\tau}=0
for
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}(n)\setminus\mathsf{PS}_{\square}(n).
Hence,

(8.26)

\tilde{x}=\sum_{(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}_{\square}(n)}(\sigma^{*}\otimes\tau^{*})(\tilde{x}_{\sigma,\tau}),\hskip 28.45274ptx_{\sigma,\tau}\in\mathsf{N}_{|\sigma|}A\otimes\mathsf{N}_{|\tau|}A^{\prime}.

(The element
\tilde{x}
is “better” than
x
because
\tilde{x}_{\sigma,\tau}=0
for
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}^{\sf D}(n)\setminus\mathsf{PS}_{\square}(n)
, while
x_{\sigma,\tau}
can be nontrivial).
Note
\rho(\tilde{x})=x
implies

(8.27)

\tilde{x}_{\sigma,\tau}=x_{\sigma,\tau},\hskip 28.45274pt\text{if}\ (\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}^{\sf N}(n).

In particular, we obtain that
x_{\sigma,\tau}=0
if
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}^{\sf N}(n)\setminus\mathsf{PS}^{\sf N}_{\square}(n).
So we only need to prove (
8.25
).

Fix some
k,l
such that
k+l=n.

Since the graph
{\bf Sh}(k,l)
is connected (Lemma
6.6
), it is enough to check (
8.25
) for two shuffles with an edge
(\mu,\nu)\to(\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
in
{\bf Sh}(k,l).
Assume that the weight of the edge
(\mu,\nu)\to(\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
is
1\leq i\leq n-1.
By Lemma
6.7
we have that
(s^{\nu}d^{i},s^{\mu}d^{i})=(s^{\nu^{\prime}}d^{i},s^{\mu^{\prime}}d^{i})\in\mathsf{PS}^{\sf N}(n)\setminus\mathsf{PS}^{\sf N}_{\square}(n).
Set
\sigma_{0}=s^{\nu}d^{i}
and
\tau_{0}=s^{\mu}d^{i}.

Since
x\in\Omega(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime})
we see that
\rho(\partial^{C}(\tilde{x}))\in\overline{B\diamond B^{\prime}}.

Combining the fact that
(\sigma_{0},\tau_{0})\in{\sf PS}^{\sf N}(n)\setminus{\sf PS}^{\sf N}_{\square}(n),
Lemma
8.3
, and Corollary
7.4

we obtain

\partial^{C}(\tilde{x})_{\sigma_{0},\tau_{0}}=\rho(\partial^{C}(\tilde{x}))_{\sigma_{0},\tau_{0}}=0.

On the other hand

(8.28)

\partial^{C}(\tilde{x})=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\sum_{(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}_{\square}(n)}(-1)^{j}d_{j}((\sigma^{*}\otimes\tau^{*})(\tilde{x}_{\sigma,\tau})).

We claim that only non-trivial summands of the sum (
8.28
) that can lie in
\sigma_{0}^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{k}A)\otimes\tau_{0}^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{l}A^{\prime})
are
(-1)^{i}d_{i}((s_{\nu}\otimes s_{\mu})(\tilde{x}_{s^{\nu},s^{\mu}}))
and
(-1)^{i}d_{i}((s_{\nu}\otimes s_{\mu})(\tilde{x}_{s^{\nu},s^{\mu}})).

Let us prove it. Take
(\sigma,\tau)\in{\sf PS}_{\square}(n).

By (
7.9
) the summand
d_{j}((\sigma^{*}\otimes\tau^{*})(\tilde{x}_{\sigma,\tau}))
is in
\varphi^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{|\varphi|}A)\otimes\psi^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{|\psi|}A^{\prime}),
where
\sigma d^{j}=\alpha\varphi
and
\tau d^{j}=\beta\psi
are epi-mono decompositions of
\sigma d^{j},\tau d^{j}.
Assume that the summand
(-1)^{j}d_{j}((\sigma\otimes\tau)(\tilde{x}_{\sigma,\tau}))
is non-trivial an lies in
\sigma_{0}^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{k}A)\otimes\tau_{0}^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{k}A^{\prime}).
Then
(\varphi,\psi)=(\sigma_{0},\tau_{0})
and
|\varphi|=k,

|\psi|=l.

We have two cases:
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}_{\square}^{\sf D}(n)
and
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}_{\square}^{\sf N}(n).
Consider them separately.

First assume
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}_{\square}^{\sf D}(n).
By Lemma
6.8
, using that
\mathsf{Ker}(\sigma d^{j})=\mathsf{Ker}(\varphi)=\mathsf{Ker}(\sigma_{0})
and
\mathsf{Ker}(\tau d^{j})=\mathsf{Ker}(\psi)=\mathsf{Ker}(\tau_{0}),
we obtain either
\mathsf{Ker}(\sigma_{0})\cap\mathsf{Ker}(\tau_{0})\neq\emptyset
or
\mathsf{Ker}(\sigma_{0})\cup\mathsf{Ker}(\tau_{0})=[n-2].
However, this contradicts to Lemma
6.7
, because
\mathsf{Ker}(\sigma_{0})\cap\mathsf{Ker}(\tau_{0})=\emptyset
and
\mathsf{Ker}(\sigma_{0})\cup\mathsf{Ker}(\tau_{0})=[n-2]\setminus\{i-1\}.

Now assume that
(\sigma,\tau)\in{\sf PS}_{\square}^{\sf N}(n).
Since
|\varphi|=k
and
|\psi|=l,
we have
\alpha:[k]\rightarrowtail[|\sigma|]
and
\beta:[l]\rightarrowtail[|\tau|].

Then
k\leq|\sigma|,l\leq|\tau|
and
k+l=n.

It follows
|\sigma|=k
and
|\tau|=l.

Therefore
\alpha={\sf id},

\beta={\sf id}
and
(\sigma,\tau)=(s^{\lambda},s^{\kappa})
for some shuffle
(\lambda,\kappa)\in{\sf Sh}(k,l)
(Lemma
6.4
).
By Lemma
6.7
we obtain that the equation
(\sigma d^{j},\tau d^{j})=(\sigma_{0},\tau_{0})
implies that
i=j
and either
(\sigma,\tau)=(s^{\nu},s^{\mu})

or
(\sigma,\tau)=(s^{\nu^{\prime}},s^{\mu^{\prime}}).
So, the only nontrivial summands lying in
\sigma_{0}^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{k}A)\otimes\tau_{0}^{*}(\mathsf{N}_{l}A^{\prime})
are
(-1)^{i}d_{i}((s_{\nu}\otimes s_{\mu})(\tilde{x}_{s^{\nu},s^{\mu}}))
and
(-1)^{i}d_{i}((s_{\nu}\otimes s_{\mu})(\tilde{x}_{s^{\nu^{\prime}},s^{\mu^{\prime}}}))
.

Therefore by (
7.9
) we have

0=\partial^{C}(\tilde{x})_{\sigma_{0},\tau_{0}}=(-1)^{i}(\tilde{x}_{s^{\nu},s^{\mu}}+\tilde{x}_{s^{\nu^{\prime}},s^{\mu^{\prime}}}).

By (
8.27
) we have
\tilde{x}_{s^{\nu},s^{\mu}}=x_{s^{\nu},s^{\mu}}
and
\tilde{x}_{s^{\nu^{\prime}},s^{\mu^{\prime}}}=x_{s^{\nu^{\prime}},s^{\mu^{\prime}}}.

The assertion follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.6
.

Since
\Omega_{n}\mathpzc{P}
and
\Omega_{n}\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}
are direct summands of
A_{n}
and
A^{\prime}_{n},
we obtain that they are also free modules. Then the short exact sequence (
8.20
) follows from the isomorphism (
8.19
) and the Künneth theorem for chain complexes. So we only need to prove the isomorphism (
8.19
). Lemma
8.8
implies that the restrictions
\varepsilon^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are well defined and
\alpha^{\prime}\varepsilon^{\prime}={\sf id}.
Lemma
8.9
implies that any element
x\in\Omega(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime})
can be presented as
x=\varepsilon(y)
for some
y\in\mathsf{N}A\otimes\mathsf{N}A^{\prime}.
Then
\varepsilon\alpha(x)=\varepsilon\alpha\varepsilon(y)=\varepsilon(y)=x
because
\alpha\varepsilon={\sf id}.
Hence
\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}={\sf id}.

∎

Corollary 8.10
.

If
\mathbb{K}
is a field, for any path pairs of vector spaces
\mathpzc{P}
and
\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}
there is an isomorphism

(8.29)

\Omega\mathpzc{P}\otimes\Omega\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}\cong\Omega(\mathpzc{P}\square\mathpzc{P}^{\prime}).

9.
Path pairs of sets and path complexes

9.1.
Path pairs of sets

A path pair of sets is a pair
\mathpzc{S}=(X,Y),
where
X
is a simplicial set and
Y
is its path subset. The associated path pair of modules is given by
\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{S}]=(\mathbb{K}[X],\mathbb{K}[Y]),
where
\mathbb{K}[-]:{\sf Set}\to{\sf Mod}
is the functor of free module applied level-wise. The complexes
\Omega(\mathpzc{S},\mathbb{K})
and
\Psi(\mathpzc{S},\mathbb{K})
are defined as

(9.1)

\Omega(\mathpzc{S},\mathbb{K})=\Omega(\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{S}]),\hskip 28.45274pt\Psi(\mathpzc{S},\mathbb{K})=\Psi(\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{S}])

If
\mathbb{K}
is fixed, we will omit it in the notation
\Omega\mathpzc{S}=\Omega(\mathpzc{S},\mathbb{K})
and
\Psi\mathpzc{S}=\Psi(\mathpzc{S},\mathbb{K}).
The path homology and copath homology of a path pair of sets are defined by
H_{*}\mathpzc{S}=H_{*}(\Omega\mathpzc{S})
and
H_{*}^{\sf c}\mathpzc{S}.
Note that there is a long exact sequence

(9.2)

{\dots}\to H_{n}\mathpzc{S}\to H_{n}X\to H_{n}^{\sf c}\mathpzc{S}\to H_{n-1}\mathpzc{S}\to{\dots}.

As in the case of path pairs of modules, motivated by Proposition
6.5
, we define the box product of two path pairs of sets
\mathpzc{S}=(X,Y)
and
\mathpzc{S}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
as

(9.3)

\mathpzc{S}\square\mathpzc{S}^{\prime}=(X\times X^{\prime},Y\diamond Y^{\prime}),

where

(9.4)

(Y\diamond Y^{\prime})_{n}=\bigcup_{k+l=n}\bigcup_{(\mu,\nu)\in{\sf Sh}(k,l)}s_{\nu}(Y_{k})\times s_{\mu}(Y_{l}^{\prime}).

Lemma 9.1
.

The subset
(Y\diamond Y^{\prime})_{n}
can be defined as

(9.5)

(Y\diamond Y^{\prime})_{n}=\bigcup_{(f,g)\in\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n)}f^{*}(Y_{|f|})\times g^{*}(Y^{\prime}_{|g|}).

and as

(9.6)

(B\diamond B^{\prime})_{n}=\bigcup_{(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathsf{PS}_{\square}(n)}\sigma^{*}(B_{|\sigma|})\times\tau^{*}(B^{\prime}_{|\tau|}).

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
8.1
.
∎

Remark 9.2
.

A more conceptual and categorical definition of the box product can be given via Day convolution (see Section
16
).

It is easy to see that

(9.7)

\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{S}\square\mathpzc{S}^{\prime}]\cong\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{S}]\square\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{S}^{\prime}].

Consider the path pair of sets given by

(9.8)

I^{\sf s}=(\Delta^{1},\Delta^{1}),\hskip 28.45274pt{\sf pt}^{\sf s}=(\Delta^{0},\Delta^{0})

and two morphisms between

i_{0},i_{1}:{\sf pt}^{\sf s}\to I^{\sf s}
them induced by
d^{0},d^{1}.

Since
\mathpzc{S}\square{\sf pt}^{\sf s}\cong\mathpzc{S},
we obtain that

(9.9)

{\sf cyl}(\mathpzc{S})=\mathpzc{S}\square I^{\sf s}

is a weak cylinder functor, and we define homotopic morphisms of path sets via this weak cylinder functor.

Proposition 9.3
.

Any homotopic morphisms of path sets
f\sim g:\mathpzc{S}\to\mathpzc{S}^{\prime}
induce homotopic maps on
\Omega
and
\Psi

(9.10)

\Omega f\sim\Omega g:\Omega\mathpzc{S}\longrightarrow\Omega\mathpzc{S}^{\prime},\hskip 28.45274pt\Psi f\sim\Psi g:\Psi\mathpzc{S}\longrightarrow\Psi\mathpzc{S}^{\prime}.

Proof.

It follows from (
9.7
), which implies the isomorphism
\mathbb{K}[{\sf cyl}(\mathpzc{S})]\cong{\sf cyl}(\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{S}]),
and Theorem
8.5
.
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

For any path pair of sets
\mathpzc{S}=(X,Y),
if we set
(A,B):=(\mathbb{K}[X],\mathbb{K}[Y]),
then
A_{n}
is a free module and
\overline{B}_{n}
is a direct summand in
A_{n}.

Proof.

The module
A_{n}=\mathbb{K}[X_{n}]
is free by the definition. Prove that
\overline{B}_{n}
is a direct summand in
A_{n}.

We set
{\sf D}_{n}X=\bigcup_{i=0}^{n-1}s_{i}(X_{n-1})
and
{\sf N}_{n}X=X_{n}\setminus{\sf D}_{n}X.
Then
{\sf N}_{n}A=\mathbb{K}[{\sf N}_{n}X]
and the map
\rho:A_{n}\to{\sf N}_{n}A
is identical on elements of
{\sf N}_{n}X
and trivial on elements of
{\sf D}_{n}X.
Therefore,
\overline{B}_{n}=\mathbb{K}[({\sf N}_{n}X)\cap Y_{n}].
The assertion follows.
∎

Theorem 9.5
.

If
\mathbb{K}
is a principal ideal domain, for any two path pairs of sets
\mathpzc{S}
and
\mathpzc{S}^{\prime}
there is an isomorphism

(9.11)

\Omega(\mathpzc{S}\square\mathpzc{S})\cong\Omega\mathpzc{S}\otimes\Omega\mathpzc{S}^{\prime}.

Moreover, there is a short exact sequence

(9.12)

0\to\bigoplus_{i+j=n}H_{i}(\mathpzc{S})\otimes H_{j}(\mathpzc{S}^{\prime})\to H_{n}(\mathpzc{S}\square\mathpzc{S}^{\prime})\to\bigoplus_{i+j=n-1}{\sf Tor}^{\mathbb{K}}_{1}(H_{i}(\mathpzc{S}),H_{j}(\mathpzc{S}^{\prime}))\to 0.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
9.4
, Theorem
8.6
and an isomorphism (
9.7
).
∎

9.2.
Regular path complexes

In this subsection we remind the definition of a regular path complex
P
and its regular complex of
\delta
-invariant paths
\Omega(P)
given in
[
13
]
. Further we show that this complex can be defined on the language of path sets.

For any set
V
we denote by
{\sf cosk}_{0}(V)
the simplicial set with components
{\sf cosk}_{0}(V)_{n}=V^{n+1},
whose face and degeneracy maps are defined by formulas

(9.13)

\begin{split}d_{i}(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})&=(v_{0},\dots,v_{i-1},v_{i+1},\dots,v_{n}),\\
s_{i}(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})&=(v_{0},\dots,v_{i},v_{i},\dots,v_{n}).\end{split}

It is easy to check that this simplicial set is the
0
-coskeleton of
V
treated as a
0
-truncated simplicial set. Note that degenerated elements of
{\sf cosk}_{0}(V)
are sequences with repetitions, which are called irregular paths in
[
13
]
. For a sequence
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})\in V^{n+1}
we set

(9.14)

\mathsf{Ker}(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})=\{0\leq i\leq n-1\mid v_{i}=v_{i+1}\}.

Then
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
is
regular
if and only if
\mathsf{Ker}(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})=\emptyset.
For arbitrary sequence
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
we denote by
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})_{\sf reg}
the regular sequence with deleted repetitions. Then

(9.15)

(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})=s_{\mu}((v_{0},\dots,v_{n})_{\sf reg}),

where
\mathsf{Ker}(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})=\{\mu_{0}<\dots<\mu_{l-1}\}.
On the other hand for any
f:[n]\to[k]
and any
(u_{0},\dots,u_{k})\in V^{k+1}
we have

(9.16)

\mathsf{Ker}(f)\subseteq\mathsf{Ker}(f^{*}(u_{0},\dots,u_{k})).

If
\mathbb{K}
is a commutative ring, then, following
[
13
]
, we set

(9.17)

\Lambda(V)=\mathbb{K}[{\sf cosk}_{0}(V)]

Degenerated elements
D(\Lambda(V))
of
\Lambda(V)
are linear combinations of irregular paths. The Moore complex
\mathsf{N}(\Lambda(V))
of this simplicial module is denoted by

(9.18)

\mathcal{R}(V)=\mathsf{N}(\Lambda(V).

Here we identify
\mathsf{N}(\Lambda(V))
with
\mathsf{C}(\Lambda(V))/\mathsf{D}(\Lambda(V)).
This complex is called the complex of regular paths. The set of all sequences
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})\in V^{n+1},v_{i}\neq v_{i+1}
forms a basis of
\mathcal{R}_{n}(V).

A
path complex
is a couple
P=(V,(P_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty})
where
V
is a set and
P_{n}\subseteq V^{n+1}
such that if
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})\in P_{n},
then
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1}),

(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\in P_{n-1}.
A sequence
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
is called
regular
, if
v_{i}\neq v_{i+1}.
A path complex is called
regular
if
P_{n}
consists of regular sequences for any
n.
Equivalently,
P
is regular, if
(v,v)\notin P_{1}
for any
v\in V.

For a regular path complex
P
we define
\mathcal{A}_{n}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{n}(V)
as the submodule generated by the images of elements from
P_{n}.
So
\mathcal{A}(P)
is a graded submodule of the chain complex
\mathcal{R}(V).
Then the complex of
\partial
-invariant forms
\Omega P
is defined as

(9.19)

\Omega P=\omega(\mathcal{R}(V),\mathcal{A}(P))

The homology of
P
is defined as the homology of
\Omega P.

9.3.
Complete path complexes

A path complex
P
is called
complete
, if

(9.20)

(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})\in P_{n}\hskip 14.22636pt\Rightarrow\hskip 14.22636pt(v_{0},\dots,v_{i},v_{i},\dots,v_{n})\in P_{n+1}.

For any path complex
P
we can consider the minimal complete path complex containing
P
with the same vertex set and denote it by
\widehat{P}.
The path complex
\widehat{P}
is called the completion of
P.

It is easy to see that a complete path complex can be defined as a path subset of
{\sf cosk}_{0}(V).
So any complete path complex
P
defines a path pair of sets

(9.21)

\mathpzc{S}P=({\sf cosk}_{0}(V),P).

Since
{\sf cosk}_{0}(V)
is contractible, the long exact sequence (
9.2
) implies that for
n\geq 1
we have an isomorphism

(9.22)

H_{n}(\mathpzc{S}P)\cong H_{n+1}^{\sf c}(\mathpzc{S}P).

Proposition 9.6
.

Let
P
be a regular path complex and let
\widehat{P}
be its completion. Then there is an isomorphism

(9.23)

\Omega P\cong\Omega(\mathpzc{S}(\widehat{P})).

Proof.

Let
(A,B)=(\mathbb{K}[{\sf cosk}_{0}(V)],\mathbb{K}[\widehat{P}]).
Then

\mathsf{N}A=\mathcal{R}(V)
and it is easy to check that
\overline{B}_{n}=\mathcal{A}_{n}(P).
Then we have
\Omega(\mathpzc{S}(\widehat{P}))=\omega(\mathcal{R}(V),\mathcal{A}(P))\cong\Omega P.

∎

9.4.
Box product of path complexes

We denote by

(9.24)

\theta:(V\times V^{\prime})^{n+1}\to V^{n+1}\times(V^{\prime})^{n+1}

the obvious bijection and
\theta_{1}:(V\times V^{\prime})^{n+1}\to V^{n+1}
and
\theta_{2}:(V\times V^{\prime})^{n+1}\to(V^{\prime})^{n+1}
are its components. For two complete path complexes
P=(V,(P_{n})),P^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},(P_{n}^{\prime})),
we define the box product
P\square P^{\prime}
as a path complex on the set
V\times V^{\prime}
such that
(P\square P^{\prime})_{n}
consists of sequences
(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})
such that

(9.25)

\theta(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})=(f^{*}(v_{0},\dots,v_{k}),g^{*}(v^{\prime}_{0},\dots,v^{\prime}_{k}))

for some
(f,g)\in\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n).
Then
\theta
restricts to a bijection

(9.26)

(P\square P^{\prime})_{n}\cong\bigcup_{(f,g)\in\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n)}f^{*}(P_{|f|})\times g^{*}(P^{\prime}_{|g|}).

By definition we obtain

(9.27)

\mathpzc{S}(P\square P^{\prime})\cong\mathpzc{S}P\square\mathpzc{S}P^{\prime}.

A sequence
(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})
of pairs
w_{i}=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i})\in W
is called
step-like
if for any
0\leq i\leq n-1
either
v_{i}=v_{i+1}
or
v^{\prime}_{i}=v^{\prime}_{i+1}
(or both). In other words, a sequence
(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})
is step-like, if
\mathsf{Ker}(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})\cup\mathsf{Ker}(v^{\prime}_{0},\dots,v^{\prime}_{n})=[n-1].

Using (
9.16
), it is easy to see that all sequences from
(P\square P^{\prime})_{n}
are step-like.

For regular path complexes
P,P^{\prime}
we define their regular box product
P\square_{\sf reg}P^{\prime}
such that
(P\square_{\sf reg}P^{\prime})_{n}
consists of regular step-like sequences
(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})
of pairs
w_{i}=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i})
such that
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})_{\sf reg}\in P_{k}
and
(v^{\prime}_{0},\dots,v^{\prime}_{n})_{\sf reg}\in P_{l}
for some
k,l\leq n.

Proposition 9.7
.

For any regular path complexes
P,P^{\prime}
we have

(9.28)

(P\square_{\sf reg}P^{\prime})^{\wedge}=\widehat{P}\square\widehat{P^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Let
(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})\in(P\square_{\sf reg}P^{\prime})_{n}
and
w_{i}=(v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i}).
Then by (
9.15
) we obtain that
\theta(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})=(s_{\mu}((v_{0},\dots,v_{n})_{\sf reg}),s_{\nu}((v^{\prime}_{0},\dots,v^{\prime}_{n})_{\sf reg})),
where
\{\mu_{0},\dots,\mu_{l-1}\}\cup\{\nu_{0},\dots,\nu_{k-1}\}=[n-1].
Therefore
(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})\in(\widehat{P}\square\widehat{P^{\prime}})_{n}.
So we proved
(P\square_{\sf reg}P^{\prime})^{\wedge}\subseteq\widehat{P}\square\widehat{P^{\prime}}.

Now assume that
(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})\in(\widehat{P}\square\widehat{P^{\prime}})_{n}.

Then

(9.29)

\theta(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})=(f^{*}(u_{0},\dots,u_{k}),g^{*}(u^{\prime}_{0},\dots,u^{\prime}_{l})),

where
(u_{0},\dots,u_{k})\in\widehat{P}_{k},

(u^{\prime}_{0},\dots,u^{\prime}_{l})\in\widehat{P^{\prime}}_{l}
and
(f,g)\in\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n).

We need to prove that
(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})_{\sf reg}\in(P\square_{\sf reg}P^{\prime})_{n}.
Note that
\theta_{i}((w_{0},\dots,w_{n})_{\sf reg})_{\sf reg}=\theta_{i}(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})_{\sf reg}
for
i=1,2.
Also note that if
(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})
is step-like, then
(w_{0},\dots,w_{n})_{\sf reg}
is also step-like. Then we only need to prove that
(f^{*}(u_{0},\dots,u_{k}))_{\sf reg}\in P_{k^{\prime}}
and
(g^{*}(u^{\prime}_{0},\dots,u^{\prime}_{l}))_{\sf reg}\in P^{\prime}_{l^{\prime}}
for some
k^{\prime},l^{\prime},
which is obvious, because they are regular sequences of
\widehat{P}
and
\widehat{P}^{\prime}
respectively. Hence
\widehat{P}\square\widehat{P^{\prime}}\subseteq(P\square P^{\prime})^{\wedge}.

∎

It is proved in
[
13
, Th.7.6]
that for any regular path complexes
P,P^{\prime}
and any field
\mathbb{K}
there is an isomorphism

(9.30)

\Omega(P\square_{\sf reg}P^{\prime})\cong\Omega P\otimes\Omega P^{\prime}.

This isomorphism follows from Proposition
9.7
, the isomorphism (
9.27
) and Theorem
9.5
, which is a corollary of Theorem
8.6
. So Theorem
8.6
can be regarded as a generalization of
[
13
, Th.7.6]
.

10.
Marked categories

10.1.
Marked categories

Mimicking the definition of marked simplicial sets
[
21
, §3.1]
we define marked categories as couples
\mathpzc{M}=(\mathpzc{C},M),
where
\mathpzc{C}
is a (small) category and
M\subseteq{\sf Mor}(\mathpzc{C})
is a set of morphisms, called marked morphisms, containing all the identity morphisms. A morphism of marked categories
(\mathpzc{C},M)\to(\mathpzc{C}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
is a functor
\mathpzc{C}\to\mathpzc{C}^{\prime}
that takes
M
to
M^{\prime}.
We denote by
Q{M}
the associated quiver, whose vertices are objects of
\mathpzc{C}
and arrows are morphisms from
M.

It is easy to check that a marked category
\mathpzc{M}=(\mathpzc{C},M)
defines a path pair of sets

(10.1)

\mathpzc{S}\mathpzc{M}=(\mathsf{Nrv}(\mathpzc{C}),{\sf P}Q{M}),

where
\mathsf{Nrv}(\mathpzc{C})
is the nerve of
\mathpzc{C}
and
{\sf P}Q{M}
is the path set associated with the quiver
Q{M},
which consists of composable sequences of marked morphisms. We also set
\Omega\mathpzc{M}=\Omega(\mathpzc{S}\mathpzc{M}),

\Psi\mathpzc{M}=\Psi(\mathpzc{S}\mathpzc{M}).
The path and copath homology of
\mathpzc{M}
are defined as

(10.2)

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathpzc{M})=H_{*}(\Omega\mathpzc{M}),\hskip 28.45274pt\mathsf{PH}_{*}^{\sf c}(\mathpzc{M})=H_{*}(\Psi\mathpzc{M}).

Then (
9.2
) implies that there is a long exact sequence

(10.3)

\dots\to\mathsf{PH}_{n}(\mathpzc{M})\to H_{n}(\mathpzc{C})\to\mathsf{PH}_{n}^{\sf c}(\mathpzc{M})\to\mathsf{PH}_{n-1}(\mathpzc{M})\to\dots

10.2.
Detailed description and low dimensions.

The nerve of a small category
\mathpzc{C}
is a simplicial set whose
n
-th component
\mathsf{Nrv}(\mathpzc{C})_{n}
consists of composable
n
-sequences of morphisms
(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
if
n\geq 1,
and
\mathsf{Nrv}(\mathpzc{C})_{0}={\sf ob}(\mathpzc{C}).
The Moore complex of the corresponding simplicial module is denoted by
\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}=\mathsf{N}(\mathbb{K}[\mathsf{Nrv}(\mathpzc{C})]).
The image of
(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
in
\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}
is denoted by
\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\rangle.

Then
(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})_{n}
is a free module freely generated by all elements
\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\rangle,
where
\gamma_{i}\neq 1_{v}
for any object
v.
If
\gamma_{i}=1_{v}
for some
i,
then
\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\rangle=0.

We also consider the maps

(10.4)

\tilde{d}_{i}:(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})_{n}\longrightarrow(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})_{n-1}

defined on the basis by the formulas

(10.5)

\begin{split}\tilde{d}_{0}\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\rangle,&=\langle\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n}\rangle\\
\tilde{d}_{i}\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\rangle&=\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{i+1}\gamma_{i},\dots,\gamma_{n}\rangle,\hskip 28.45274pt1\leq i\leq n-1,\\
\tilde{d}_{n}\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\rangle&=\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n-1}\rangle,\end{split}

if
n\geq 2,
and
\tilde{d}_{0}(\alpha)={\sf codom}(\alpha)
and
\tilde{d}_{1}(\alpha)={\sf dom}(\alpha)
for
n=1.

By the definition the
differential
\partial^{\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}}
on
\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}
is induced by the differential
on
\mathbb{K}[\mathsf{Nrv}(\mathpzc{C})],
which is defined as
\sum(-1)^{i}d_{i}.

It is not difficult to check that the differential on
\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}
satisfies

(10.6)

\partial^{\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\tilde{d}_{i}.

For a marked category
\mathpzc{M}=(\mathpzc{C},M)
we set

(10.7)

\overline{\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q{M}]}=\rho(\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q{M}]).

Generalising the definition of
\mathsf{D}A
and
\mathsf{N}A
for a simplicial module
A
to a path module
B,
we consider a graded modules
\mathsf{D}B
and
\mathsf{N}B
defined by the formulas

(10.8)

(\mathsf{D}B)_{n}=\sum_{i}s_{i}(B_{n-1}),\hskip 28.45274pt\mathsf{N}B=B/\mathsf{D}B.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
\mathpzc{M}=(\mathpzc{C},M)
be a marked category and we set
A=\mathbb{K}[\mathsf{Nrv}(\mathpzc{C})]
and
B=\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q{M}].
Then

(10.9)

(\mathsf{D}B)_{n}=(\mathsf{D}A)_{n}\cap B_{n}.

Proof.

The inclusion
\subseteq
is obvious. Prove
\supseteq.

The set of all
n
-sequences of
composable morphisms is a basis of
A_{n}.
The set of
n
-sequences containing an
identity morphism is a basis of
(\mathsf{D}A)_{n}.
The set of all
n
-sequences of composable morphisms from
M
is a basis of

B_{n}.
So
(\mathsf{D}A)_{n}
and
B_{n}
are generated by subsets of the basis of
A_{n}.

So, the intersection of these subsets is a basis of the
intersection
(\mathsf{D}A)_{n}\cap B_{n}.
Then the set of all
n
-sequences of composable marked
morphisms containing an identity morphism is a basis of the intersection
(\mathsf{D}A)_{n}\cap B_{n}.
All elements of this basis
are in
(\mathsf{D}B)_{n},
which implies the inclusion
\supseteq.

∎

If we set
\mathsf{N}M:=\mathsf{N}(\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q{M}]),
we obtain that Lemma
10.1
implies that
\rho
induces an isomorphism

(10.10)

\mathsf{N}M\cong\overline{\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q{M}]}.

We will identify
\mathsf{N}M
with its image in
\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}.
Then

(10.11)

\Omega\mathpzc{M}=\omega(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C},\mathsf{N}M),\hskip 28.45274pt\Psi\mathpzc{M}=\psi(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C},\mathsf{N}M).

Proposition 10.2

(cf.
[
13
, Prop. 4.2]
)
.

For any marked category
\mathpzc{M}
we have

(\Omega\mathpzc{M})_{n}=(\mathsf{N}M)_{n}
for
n=0,1
and

(10.12)

(\Omega\mathpzc{M})_{2}=(\mathsf{N}M)_{2}\cap\tilde{d}_{1}^{-1}((\mathsf{N}M)_{1}).

Moreover,
(\Omega\mathpzc{M})_{2}
is generated by differences of composable pairs
\langle\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\rangle-\langle\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\rangle
such that
\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}=\beta_{2}\alpha_{2}
and
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in M.

Proof.

The equation for
n=0,1
are obvious. For
n=2
we have
\partial_{2}^{\mathsf{N}M}=\tilde{d}_{0}-\tilde{d}_{1}+\tilde{d}_{2}.
For any
x\in(\mathsf{N}M)_{2}
we have
\tilde{d}_{0}(x),\tilde{d}_{2}(x)\in(\mathsf{N}M)_{1}.
Hence
\partial^{\mathsf{N}M}_{2}(x)\in(\mathsf{N}M)_{1}
if and only if
\tilde{d}_{1}(x)\in(\mathsf{N}M)_{1}.
The equation (
10.12
) follows.

Denote by
D\subseteq(\mathsf{N}M)_{2}
the submodule generated by
\langle\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\rangle-\langle\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\rangle
for
\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}=\beta_{2}\alpha_{2}.
It is easy to see that
D\subseteq(\Omega\mathpzc{M})_{2}.
Prove that
(\Omega\mathpzc{M})_{2}\subseteq D.
Let
x\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\langle\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\rangle\in(\Omega\mathpzc{M})_{2},
where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{K}\setminus\{0\}
and
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in M\setminus\{1_{c}|c\in{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{C})\}.
We want to prove
x\in D
by induction on
n.
For
n=0
this is obvious. Assume that
n\geq 1.
Then
\tilde{d}_{1}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\langle\beta_{i}\alpha_{i}\rangle=0.
If
\beta_{n}\alpha_{n}=1_{v}
for some object
v
then
\langle\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}\rangle=\langle\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}\rangle-\langle 1_{v},1_{v}\rangle\in D
and by induction hypothesis
x-a_{n}\langle\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}\rangle\in D.
Hence
x\in D.
So we can assume that
\beta_{n}\alpha_{n}\neq 1_{v}.
Thus
a_{n}\langle\beta_{n}\alpha_{n}\rangle\neq 0,
and hence,
n\geq 2.
Then there exists
1\leq m<n
such that
\beta_{m}\alpha_{m}=\beta_{n}\alpha_{n}.
It follows that
x-a_{n}(\langle\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}\rangle-\langle\alpha_{m},\beta_{m}\rangle)\in D
by the induction hypothesis. Hence
x\in D.

∎

10.3.
DG-coalgebra structure on
\Omega

Consider the composition of the map induced by the diadonal
\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}\to\mathsf{N}(\mathpzc{C}\times\mathpzc{C})
and the Alexander-Whitney map
\alpha:\mathsf{N}(\mathpzc{C}\times\mathpzc{C})\to\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}\otimes\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}

(10.13)

\nu:\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}\longrightarrow\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}\otimes\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}.

This map
\nu_{n}:\mathsf{N}_{n}\mathpzc{C}\to(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}\otimes\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})_{n}
can be written explicitly on the basis as
\nu_{n}=\sum_{k+l=n}\nu_{k,l},
where

(10.14)

\nu_{k,l}:\mathsf{N}_{n}\mathpzc{C}\to\mathsf{N}_{k}\mathpzc{C}\otimes\mathsf{N}_{l}\mathpzc{C}

is defined by the formulas

(10.15)

\begin{split}\nu_{0,n}(\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\rangle)&=1_{v_{0}}\otimes\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\rangle\\
\nu_{k,l}(\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\rangle)&=\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{i}\rangle\otimes\langle\alpha_{i+1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\rangle,\hskip 28.45274ptk,l\geq 1\\
\nu_{n,0}(\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\rangle)&=\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\rangle\otimes 1_{v_{n}}.\end{split}

We also consider the map
e:\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}\to(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})_{0}\to\mathbb{K}
given by
e(1_{v})=1.
It is easy to see that
\nu,e
define a structure of dg-coalgebra on
\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}.

Proposition 10.3
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a principal ideal domain and
\mathpzc{M}
be a marked category. Then
\Omega\mathpzc{M}
is a split sub-dg-coalgebra of
\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}
.

Proof.

It is easy to check that
\mathsf{N}M\subseteq\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}
is a split subcoalgebra. Then the assertion follows from Proposition
3.15
.
∎

Theorem 10.4
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a principal ideal domain and
k,l
are natural numbers. Then for any marked category
\mathpzc{M}
the map
\nu_{k,l}
induces a monomorphism

(10.16)

\Omega_{k+l}\mathpzc{M}\rightarrowtail\Omega_{k}\mathpzc{M}\otimes\Omega_{l}\mathpzc{M}.

Proof.

The map
\nu_{k,l}:\mathsf{N}_{k+l}\mathpzc{C}\to\mathsf{N}_{k}\mathpzc{C}\otimes\mathsf{N}_{l}\mathpzc{C}
is a monomorphism because it sends different elements of the basis to different elements of the basis. Proposition
10.3
implies that it can be restricted to the map
\Omega_{k+l}\mathpzc{M}\rightarrowtail\Omega_{k}\mathpzc{M}\otimes\Omega_{l}\mathpzc{M},
which is also a monomorphism.
∎

Corollary 10.5

(cf.
[
13
, Prop. 3.23]
)
.

If
\mathbb{K}
is a principal ideal domain,
\mathpzc{M}
is a marked category and
\Omega_{n}\mathpzc{M}=0
for some
n,
then for any
m>n
we also have
\Omega_{m}\mathpzc{M}=0.

Corollary 10.6
.

If
\mathbb{K}
is a field and
\mathpzc{M}
is a marked category, then

(10.17)

{\sf dim}(\Omega_{k+l}\mathpzc{M})\leq{\sf dim}(\Omega_{k}\mathpzc{M})\cdot{\sf dim}(\Omega_{l}\mathpzc{M})

for any natural
k,l.

10.4.
Cohomology of marked categories, cup product

For a
\mathbb{K}
-module
V
we set
M^{\vee}={\sf Hom}(V,\mathbb{K}).
For any category
\mathpzc{C}
we consider the cochain complex
(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})^{\vee}.
The cochain complex has a natural structure of dg-algebra defined by the composition

(10.18)

(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})^{\vee}\otimes(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})^{\vee}\longrightarrow(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C}\otimes\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})^{\vee}\overset{\nu^{\vee}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})^{\vee}.

Similarly we can define a graded algebra structure (without a differential) on
(\mathsf{N}M)^{\vee}.
For a marked category
\mathpzc{M}=(\mathpzc{C},M)
we obtain a homomorphism of graded algebras
(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})^{\vee}\to(\mathsf{N}M)^{\vee},
whose kernel is denoted by
K\mathpzc{M}.
Then
K\mathpzc{M}
is an graded ideal of
(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})^{\vee}.
We set

(10.19)

\Omega^{\bullet}\mathpzc{M}=\psi((\mathsf{N}C)^{\vee},K\mathpzc{M})

and define the path cohomology of
\mathpzc{M}
by the formula

(10.20)

\mathsf{PH}^{*}(\mathpzc{M}):=H^{*}(\Omega^{\bullet}\mathpzc{M}).

By Proposition
3.14

\Omega^{\bullet}\mathpzc{M}
inherits a natural structure of a dg-algebra. Hence
\mathsf{PH}^{*}(\mathpzc{M})
has a natural structure of a graded algebra. Note that this structure is defined for any commutative ring
\mathbb{K}.

The construction is natural and any morphism of marked categories
\mathpzc{M}\to\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}
defines a homomorphism of graded algebras

(10.21)

\mathsf{PH}^{*}(\mathpzc{M}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathsf{PH}^{*}(\mathpzc{M}).

In particular, for any marked category
\mathpzc{M}=(\mathpzc{C},M)
we have a homomorphism from the cohomology algebra of the category to the cohomology of the marked category
H^{*}(\mathpzc{C})\to\mathsf{PH}^{*}(\mathpzc{M}).

Similarly to Theorem
10.4
we obtain that the product on
\Omega^{\bullet}\mathpzc{M}
defines an epimorphism

(10.22)

\Omega^{k}\mathpzc{M}\otimes\Omega^{l}\mathpzc{M}\twoheadrightarrow\Omega^{k+l}\mathpzc{M}

for any natural
k,l.
If
\mathbb{K}
is a field, then by Proposition
3.16
we obtain that

(10.23)

\Omega^{\bullet}\mathpzc{M}\cong(\Omega\mathpzc{M})^{\vee},\hskip 28.45274pt\mathsf{PH}^{*}(\mathpzc{M})\cong(\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathpzc{M}))^{\vee}.

10.5.
Box product of marked categories

We define the box product of marked categories
\mathpzc{M}=(\mathpzc{C},M)
and
\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}=(\mathpzc{C}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
by the formula

(10.24)

\mathpzc{M}\square\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}=(\mathpzc{C}\times\mathpzc{C}^{\prime},\{(\mu,1_{c^{\prime}})\}\cup\{(1_{c},\mu^{\prime})\}),

where
\{(\mu,1_{c^{\prime}})\}
stands for

\{(\mu,1_{c^{\prime}})\mid\mu\in M,c\in{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{C}^{\prime})\}
and
\{(1_{c},\mu^{\prime})\}
stands for
\{(1_{c},\mu^{\prime})\}=\{(1_{c},\mu^{\prime})\mid c\in{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{C}),\mu^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}\}.
It is easy to see that

(10.25)

Q_{\mathpzc{M}\square\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}}\cong Q_{\mathpzc{M}}\Box Q_{\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}}.

Proposition
6.5
implies that the box product is compatible with the box product

(10.26)

\mathpzc{S}(\mathpzc{M}\square\mathpzc{M}^{\prime})\cong\mathpzc{S}\mathpzc{M}\square\mathpzc{S}\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}.

Proposition 10.7
.

If
\mathbb{K}
is a principal ideal domain, for any marked categories
\mathpzc{M},\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}
we have

(10.27)

\Omega(\mathpzc{M}\square\mathpzc{M}^{\prime})\cong\Omega\mathpzc{M}\otimes\Omega\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}.

Moreover, if we set
H_{*}=\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathpzc{M}),

H^{\prime}_{*}=\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathpzc{M}^{\prime})
and
H^{\prime\prime}_{*}=\mathsf{PH}(\mathpzc{M}\times\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}),
we get a Künneth-like short exact sequence

(10.28)

0\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i+j=n}H_{i}\otimes H^{\prime}_{j}\longrightarrow H^{\prime\prime}_{n}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i+j=n-1}{\sf Tor}^{\mathbb{K}}_{1}(H_{i},H^{\prime}_{j})\longrightarrow 0.

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
9.5
and (
10.26
).
∎

10.6.
Homotopy invariance for marked categories

Consider a marked category that models the interval
I^{\sf m}=(\mathpzc{F}(\mathsf{q}^{1}),\mathsf{q}^{1}_{1}),
where
\mathsf{q}^{1}
is the quiver with two vertices
(0\to 1)
and
\mathpzc{F}(\mathsf{q}^{1})
is the free category defined by the quiver with only one non-identical morphism. We also consider the marked category that models a point
{\sf pt}^{\sf m}=(\mathpzc{F}(\mathsf{q}^{0}),\mathsf{q}^{0}_{1}).
Then for any marked category
\mathpzc{M}
we have
\mathpzc{M}\square{\sf pt}^{\sf m}\cong\mathpzc{M}.

Hence two morphisms
i^{0},i^{1}:{\sf pt}^{\sf m}\rightrightarrows I^{\sf m}

define a weak cylinder functor

{\sf cyl}(\mathpzc{M})=\mathpzc{M}\square I^{\sf m},

and we define homotopic morphisms of marked categories via this weak cylinder functor.

Proposition 10.8
.

Two morphisms of marked categories
f,g:(\mathpzc{C},M)\to(\mathpzc{C}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
are one-step homotopic if and only if there is a natural transformation
\varphi:f\to g
such that
\varphi_{c}\in M^{\prime}
for any object
c
of
\mathpzc{C}.

Proof.

The set of natural transformations
\varphi:f\to g
is in bijection with the set of functors
h:\mathpzc{C}\times\mathpzc{F}(\mathsf{q}^{1})\to\mathpzc{C}^{\prime}
such that
h(\alpha,{\sf id}_{0})=f(\alpha),

h(\alpha,{\sf id}_{1})=g(\alpha)
for any morphism
\alpha
of
\mathpzc{C}.
The functor
h
corresponding to
\varphi
is defined by the formula
h({\sf id}_{c},(0,1))=\varphi_{c}.
The assumptions
f(M)\subseteq M^{\prime},g(M)\subseteq M^{\prime}
and
\varphi_{c}\in M^{\prime}
are equivalent to the fact that this morphism defines a morphism of marked categories
h:\mathpzc{M}\square I^{\sf m}\to\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}
such that
hi^{0}{M}=f
and
hi^{1}{M}=g.
The assertion follows.
∎

Proposition 10.9
.

Any two homotopic morphisms of marked categories
f\sim g:\mathpzc{M}\to\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}
induce homotopic morphisms of complexes

(10.29)

\Omega f\sim\Omega g:\Omega\mathpzc{M}\to\Omega\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}\hskip 28.45274pt\Psi f\sim\Psi g:\Psi\mathpzc{M}\to\Psi\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}.

Proof.

Then the assertion follows from Proposition
9.3
, (
10.26
) and the formulas
\mathpzc{S}(I^{\sf m})=I^{\sf s}
and
\mathpzc{S}({\sf pt}^{\sf m})={\sf pt}^{\sf s}.

∎

10.7.
Isomorphism-lemma for marked categories

For a marked category
\mathpzc{M}=(\mathpzc{C},M)
we denote by
M^{2}
the set of compositions
\mu\mu^{\prime},
where
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are composable morphisms from
M.
Since all identity morphism are in
M,
we obtain
M\subseteq M^{2}.

Proposition 10.10

(Isomorphism-lemma)
.

Let
\mathpzc{M}=(\mathpzc{C},M)
and
\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}=(\mathpzc{C}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
be marked categories and
f:\mathpzc{M}\to\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}
be a morphism of marked categories, which induces a bijection on objects and bijections
M\cong M^{\prime}
and
M^{2}\cong(M^{\prime})^{2}.
Then
f
induces an isomorphism

(10.30)

\Omega\mathpzc{M}\cong\Omega\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}.

Proof.

Set
A=\mathbb{K}[\mathsf{Nrv}(\mathpzc{C})],

B=\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q{M}]
and
E=\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q_{\mathpzc{M}^{[2]}}],
where
\mathpzc{M}^{[2]}=(\mathpzc{C},M^{2}).
As usual, we also denote by
\overline{B}
the image of
B\to\mathsf{N}A
and by
\overline{E}
the image of
E\to\mathsf{N}A.
We use similar notation for
(\mathpzc{C}^{\prime},M^{\prime}):

A^{\prime},B^{\prime},E^{\prime}
. The fact that
f
induces isomorphisms
M\cong M^{\prime}
and
M^{2}\cong(M^{\prime})^{2}
implies that
f
induces isomorphisms
B\cong B^{\prime}
and
E\cong E^{\prime}.
Therefore,
f
induces isomorphisms
\mathsf{N}B\cong\mathsf{N}B^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{N}E\cong\mathsf{N}E^{\prime}.
By Lemma
10.1
we see that
\mathsf{N}B\cong\overline{B},

\mathsf{N}E\cong\overline{E}
and
\mathsf{N}B^{\prime}\cong\overline{B}^{\prime},

\mathsf{N}E^{\prime}\cong\overline{E}^{\prime}.
Then
f
induces isomorphisms
\overline{B}\cong\overline{B}^{\prime}
and
\overline{E}\cong\overline{E}^{\prime}.
By the definitions of
M^{2}
we see that
\partial(\overline{B})\subseteq\overline{E}.
Similarly
\partial(\overline{B}^{\prime})\subseteq\overline{E}^{\prime}.
Then the assertion follows from Proposition
3.6
.
∎

Corollary 10.11
.

If
\mathpzc{C}
is a subcategory of
\mathpzc{C}^{\prime},
then

(10.31)

\Omega(\mathpzc{C},M)\cong\Omega(\mathpzc{C}^{\prime},M).

10.8.
Categories with ideals and free categories

If
\mathpzc{C}
is a small category, a set
I\subseteq{\sf mor}(\mathpzc{C})
is called ideal, if a composition of a morphism from
I
with any other morphism, from any side, is also from
I.

Proposition 10.12
.

Let
\mathpzc{M}=(\mathpzc{C},M)
be a marked category and let
I
be an ideal of
\mathpzc{C}
such that
M\cap I=\emptyset
and the composition of any two non-identical arrows from
M
are in
I.
Then

(10.32)

\Omega_{n}\mathpzc{M}=\{x\in(\mathsf{N}M)_{n}\mid\tilde{d}_{i}(x)=0,\ \text{for}\ 1\leq i\leq n-1\}.

The differential
\partial:\Omega_{n}\mathpzc{M}\to\Omega_{n-1}\mathpzc{M}
is given by
\partial=\tilde{d}_{0}+(-1)^{n}\tilde{d}_{n}.

Proof.

Denote by
E_{n-1,i}
a submodule of
(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{C})_{n-1}
which is generated by composable
(n-1)
-sequences of non-identical morphisms
\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n-1}\rangle
such that
\gamma_{i}\in I
and
\gamma_{j}\notin I
for
j\neq i.

We also denote by
E_{n-1}
the sum of the modules
E_{n-1,i}.

It is easy to see that
E_{n-1}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n-1}E_{n-1,i}.
Also note that
\tilde{d}_{i}((\mathsf{N}M)_{n})\subseteq E_{n-1,i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
\tilde{d}_{0}((\mathsf{N}M)_{n}),\tilde{d}_{n}((\mathsf{N}M)_{n})\subseteq(\mathsf{N}M)_{n-1}.
Moreover, note that
E_{n-1}\cap(\mathsf{N}M)_{n-1}=0.
It is easy to see that
\tilde{d}_{0}((\mathsf{N}M)_{n}),\tilde{d}_{n}((\mathsf{N}M)_{n})\subseteq(\mathsf{N}M)_{n-1}.
So for
b\in(\mathsf{N}M)_{n}

(10.33)

\partial^{\mathsf{N}M}(b)=\big(\tilde{d}_{0}(b)+(-1)^{n}\tilde{d}_{n}(b)\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}\tilde{d}_{i}(b).

Therefore
\partial(b)\in(\mathsf{N}M)_{n-1}
if and only if
\tilde{d}_{i}(b)=0
for
1\leq i\leq n-1.

∎

Proposition 10.13
.

Let
Q
be a quiver and
\mathpzc{F}(Q)
be the free category (the category of paths) generated by
Q.
Then

(10.34)

\Omega_{n}(\mathpzc{F}(Q),Q_{1})=0,\hskip 28.45274ptn\geq 2

and
\Omega_{n}(\mathpzc{F}(Q),Q_{1})\cong\mathbb{K}^{Q_{n}}
for
n=0,1.
Moreover,
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathpzc{F}(Q),Q_{1})
is isomorphic to the homology of the quiver
Q
treated as
1
-dimensional space.

Proof.

We denote by
I
the ideal of paths of length
\geq 2.
Then it follows from Proposition
10.12
and the fact that for the free category the maps
\tilde{d}_{i}:\mathsf{N}(\mathpzc{F}(Q))_{n}\to\mathsf{N}(\mathpzc{F}(Q))_{n-1}
are injective for
1\leq i\leq n-1.

∎

10.9.
Digraphs as marked categories

By a digraph
G
we mean a couple of sets
G=(V,E),
where
E\subseteq V^{2}
such that
(v,v)\in E
for any
v\in V.

The edges of the form
(v,v)
are called degenerated.
A digraph
G
defines a quiver
Q(G)
such that
Q(G)_{0}=V,

Q(G)_{1}=E
and
t(v,v^{\prime})=v,h(v,v^{\prime})=v^{\prime},s(v)=(v,v).
We also consider a category
{\sf c}(V)
such that
{\sf ob}({\sf c}(V))=V
and
{\sf c}(V)(v,v^{\prime})=\{(v,v^{\prime})\}.
So a digraph
G
defines a marked category
\mathpzc{M}G=({\sf c}(V),E),
which defines a chain complex
\Omega G=\Omega(\mathpzc{M}G)
and the homology
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G)=H_{*}(\Omega G).
It is easy to see that this chain complex coincides with the chain complex defined in
[
13
]
. Proposition
10.7
implies that
\Omega(G\square G^{\prime})\cong\Omega G\otimes\Omega G^{\prime},
if
\mathbb{K}
is a principal ideal domain.
Proposition
10.9
implies that homotopic morphisms of graphs
f\sim g:G\to G^{\prime}
induce homotopic morphisms of complexes
\Omega f\sim\Omega g:\Omega G\to\Omega G^{\prime}
and
\Psi f\sim\Psi g:\Psi G\to\Psi G^{\prime}.

Since, the category
{\sf c}(V)
is contractible, we see that
\mathsf{PH}_{n}(G)\cong\mathsf{PH}^{\sf c}_{n+1}(G)
for
n\geq 1.
Also note that if
\mathbb{K}
is a field, then Corollary
10.6
implies that

(10.35)

{\sf dim}(\Omega_{k+l}G)\leq{\sf dim}(\Omega_{k}G)\cdot{\sf dim}(\Omega_{l}G).

11.
A generalization: marked linear categories

A (
\mathbb{K}
-)
linear category
is a category
\mathpzc{A}
enriched over the category of
\mathbb{K}
-modules i.e.
\mathpzc{A}
is a category together with a structure of
\mathbb{K}
-module on the hom-set
\mathpzc{A}(a,a^{\prime})
for any objects
a,a^{\prime}
such that the composition is bilinear. A linear functor between linear categories is a functor which is a homomorphism on any hom-set.

We denote by
\mathbb{K}^{c}
the linear category with one object whose hom-set is equal to
\mathbb{K}
and the composition is defined by multiplication. An
augmented linear category

\mathpzc{A}
is a linear category together with a linear functor
\varepsilon:\mathpzc{A}\to\mathbb{K}^{c}.
We denote by
\mathpzc{A}^{1}
the wide subcategory of
\mathpzc{A}
whose morphisms are all morphisms
\alpha
satisfying
\varepsilon(\alpha)=1.

Any category
\mathpzc{C}
defines an augmented linear category
\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{C}]
. The category
\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{C}]
has the same objects and its hom-sets are free modules generated by the hom-sets of
\mathpzc{C}:

\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{C}](c,c^{\prime})=\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{C}(c,c^{\prime})].
The composition on
\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{C}]
is defined as the bilinear extension of the composition on
\mathpzc{C}.
The augmentation
\varepsilon:\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{C}]\to\mathbb{K}^{c}
is defined so that for any morphism
\alpha
of
\mathpzc{C}
we have
\varepsilon(\alpha)=1.
In particular, we have
\mathpzc{C}\subseteq\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{C}]^{1}.

A
linear nerve
of an augmented linear category is a simplicial module
{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A})
such that

(11.1)

{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A})_{0}=\mathbb{K}[{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{A})]

and

(11.2)

{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A})_{n}=\bigoplus_{a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{A})}\mathpzc{A}(a_{0},a_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes\mathpzc{A}(a_{n-1},a_{n})

for
n\geq 1.
The face maps
d_{i}:{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A})_{n}\to{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A})_{n-1}
and degeneracy maps
s_{i}:{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A})_{n-1}\to{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A})_{n}
for
n\geq 2
are defined by

(11.3)

\begin{split}d_{0}(\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{n})&=\varepsilon(\alpha_{1})\alpha_{2}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{n},\\
d_{i}(\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{n})&=\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{i+1}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{n},\hskip 28.45274pt1\leq i\leq n-1,\\
d_{n}(\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{n})&=\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{n-1}\varepsilon(\alpha_{n}),\\
s_{i}(\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{n-1})&=\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\dots\otimes 1_{a_{i}}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{n-1},\hskip 28.45274pt0\leq i\leq n-1,\end{split}

where
\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{i+1}=\alpha_{i+1}\circ\alpha_{i}.

For
n=1
the face and degeneracy maps are defined by

(11.4)

d_{0}(\alpha)=\varepsilon(\alpha)h(\alpha),\hskip 28.45274ptd_{1}(\alpha)=t(\alpha)\varepsilon(\alpha),\hskip 28.45274pts_{0}(a)={\sf id}_{a}.

Note that all the maps
d_{i}
are homomorphisms (thank to the augmentation
\varepsilon
in the formulas). The fact that it is a simplicial module is straightforward.

It is easy to see that for a category
\mathpzc{C}
we have

(11.5)

{\sf Lnerve}(\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{C}])\cong\mathbb{K}[\mathsf{Nrv}(\mathpzc{C})].

A
marked linear category
is a couple

(11.6)

\mathpzc{L}=(\mathpzc{A},M),

where
\mathpzc{A}
is an small augmented linear category and
M\subseteq\mathpzc{A}^{1}
is a subset containing all identity morphisms, such that for any
a,a^{\prime}\in{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{A})
, if we set
M(a,a^{\prime})=M\cap\mathpzc{A}(a,a^{\prime})
, the map
\mathbb{K}[M(a,a^{\prime})]\to\mathpzc{A}(a,a^{\prime})
is a split monomorphism (if
\mathbb{K}
is a field, this is equivalent to linear independence of
M(a,a^{\prime})
). We denote by
Q{L}
the quiver whose vertices are objects of
\mathpzc{L},
and the set of arrows is
M.
Therefore the path set
\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q{L}]
is embedded into
{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A})
and each map
\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q{L}]_{n}\to{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A})_{n}
is a split monomorphism. We identify
\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q{L}]
with its image in
{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A}).
So we can consider a path pair of modules

(11.7)

\mathpzc{P}\mathpzc{L}=({\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A}),\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q{L}])

and set

(11.8)

\Omega\mathpzc{L}=\Omega(\mathpzc{P}\mathpzc{L}),\hskip 28.45274pt\Psi\mathpzc{L}=\Psi(\mathpzc{P}\mathpzc{L}).

The tensor product
\mathpzc{A}\otimes\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
of two linear categories
\mathpzc{A}
and
\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
is defined so that
{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{A}\otimes\mathpzc{A}^{\prime})={\sf ob}(\mathpzc{A})\times{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{A}^{\prime})
and
(\mathpzc{A}\otimes\mathpzc{A}^{\prime})((a,a^{\prime}),(b,b^{\prime}))=\mathpzc{A}(a,b)\otimes\mathpzc{A}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}).
If
\mathpzc{A}
and
\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
are augmented, then
\mathpzc{A}\otimes\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
inherits an obvious augmentation. The box product of two marked linear categories
\mathpzc{L}=(\mathpzc{A},M)
and
\mathpzc{L}^{\prime}=(\mathpzc{A}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
is defined by the formula

(11.9)

\mathpzc{L}\square\mathpzc{L}^{\prime}=(\mathpzc{A}\otimes\mathpzc{A}^{\prime},\{\mu\otimes 1_{a^{\prime}}\}\cup\{1_{a}\otimes\mu^{\prime}\}),

where
\{\mu\otimes 1_{a^{\prime}}\}
stands for
\{\mu\otimes 1_{a^{\prime}}\mid\mu\in M,a^{\prime}\in{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{A}^{\prime})\}
and
\{1_{a}\otimes\mu^{\prime}\}
stands for
\{1_{a}\otimes\mu^{\prime}\mid a\in{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{A}),\mu^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}\}.
It is easy to see that
{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A}\otimes\mathpzc{A}^{\prime})\cong{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A})\otimes{\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A}^{\prime})
and
Q_{\mathpzc{L}\square\mathpzc{L}^{\prime}}\cong Q{L}\square Q_{\mathpzc{L}^{\prime}}.
Using this and Proposition
6.5
, we obtain

(11.10)

\mathpzc{P}(\mathpzc{L}\square\mathpzc{L}^{\prime})\cong\mathpzc{P}\mathpzc{L}\square\mathpzc{P}\mathpzc{L}^{\prime}.

A morphism of marked linear categories
f:(\mathpzc{A},M)\to(\mathpzc{A}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
is a morphism of augmented linear categories
f:\mathpzc{A}\to\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
such that
f(M)\subseteq M^{\prime}.
As usual we consider a model of an interval
I^{\sf lm}=(\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{F}(\mathsf{q}^{1})],\mathsf{q}^{1}_{1}),
a model of a point
{\sf pt}^{\sf lm}=(\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{F}(\mathsf{q}^{0})],\mathsf{q}^{0}_{1}),
define a weak cylinder functor

(11.11)

{\sf cyl}(\mathpzc{L})=\mathpzc{L}\square I^{\sf lm},

and define homotopic morphisms via this weak cylinder functor. The equations (
11.10
) and
\mathpzc{P}(I^{\sf lm})=I^{\sf p}
imply that homotopic morphisms
f\sim g:\mathpzc{L}\to\mathpzc{L}^{\prime}
induce homotopic morphisms of complexes

(11.12)

\Omega f\sim\Omega g:\Omega\mathpzc{L}\to\Omega\mathpzc{L}^{\prime},\hskip 28.45274pt\Psi f\sim\Psi g:\Psi\mathpzc{L}\to\Psi\mathpzc{L}^{\prime}.

12.
k
-power homology of quivers

In this section we show an approach to homology of quivers developed in
[
18
]
via marked linear categories.

12.1.
Definition via marked linear categories

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative ring and
k\geq 1
be a natural number such that
k\cdot 1_{\mathbb{K}}
is invertible in
\mathbb{K}.
For a set
V
and a natural number
k\geq 1
we denote by
{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}
a quiver with the set of vertices
V
and with exactly
k
non-degenerated edges from
v
to
v^{\prime}
for any
v,v^{\prime}\in V.
The non-degenerated edges from
v
to
v^{\prime}
are denoted by
\alpha_{i}^{v,v^{\prime}}
for
1\leq i\leq k.
We denote by
\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V}
the augmented linear category such that
{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V})=V
and
\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V}(v,v^{\prime})=\mathbb{K}[{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}(v,v^{\prime})].
The composition is defined by the formula

(12.1)

\alpha^{v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}}_{n}\circ\alpha^{v,v^{\prime}}_{m}=\zeta_{v,v^{\prime\prime}},

where
\zeta_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}
is defined as the average of all non-degenerate edges

(12.2)

\zeta_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}^{v,v^{\prime\prime}},

for any
1\leq n,m\leq k.
It is easy to see that

(12.3)

\alpha^{v^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime\prime\prime}}_{n}\circ(\alpha^{v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}}_{m}\circ\alpha^{v,v^{\prime}}_{l})=\zeta_{v,v^{\prime\prime\prime}}=(\alpha^{v^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime\prime\prime}}_{n}\circ\alpha^{v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}}_{m})\circ\alpha^{v,v^{\prime}}_{l},

so the composition is associative. The augmentation
\varepsilon:\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V}\to\mathbb{K}^{c}
is defined so that
\varepsilon(\alpha_{i}^{v,v^{\prime}})=1.
Note that the composition (
12.1
) is compatible with the augmentation
\varepsilon(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}^{v,v^{\prime\prime}})=1
i.e.
\varepsilon
is a functor.

The power of a quiver
Q
is the supremum of cardinalities of
Q(v,v^{\prime})
for all
v,v^{\prime}\in Q_{0}.

Now assume that
Q
is a quiver such that
Q_{0}=V
and for any
v,v^{\prime}\in V
the cardinality of
Q(v,v^{\prime})
is at most
k.
So there is an embedding

(12.4)

i:Q\rightarrowtail{\sf Q}_{V}^{k},

which induces an embedding
i{A}:Q\rightarrowtail(\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V})^{1}.
Therefore we can consider a marked linear category and the corresponding complex

(12.5)

\mathpzc{L}(i)=(\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V},i{A}(Q_{1})),\hskip 28.45274pt\Omega(i)=\Omega(\mathpzc{L}(i)).

By the definition the homology depends on the embedding. However, they don’t really depend on the embedding (up to
canonical
isomorphism). Indeed, for any two embeddings
i_{1},i_{2}:Q\rightarrowtail{\sf Q}_{V}^{k}
there exists an automorphism
\varphi:{\sf Q}_{V}^{k}\to{\sf Q}_{V}^{k}
such that the diagram is commutative

(12.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf Q}_{V}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf Q}_{V}^{k}}
\leftarrowtail
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}}
\leftarrowtail
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

Hence
\varphi
defines an isomorphism

(12.7)

\varphi_{*}:\Omega(i_{1})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\Omega(i_{2}).

Moreover, this isomorphism does not depend of
\varphi,

because if we have two different automorphisms
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}
such that
\varphi i_{1}=i_{2}

and
\varphi^{\prime}i_{1}=i_{2},
the maps
\varphi_{*},\varphi^{\prime}_{*}
coincide by Proposition
3.5

(12.8)

\varphi_{*}=\varphi^{\prime}_{*}:\Omega(i_{1})\to\Omega(i_{2}).

So the isomorphism is uniquely defined by
i_{1}
and
i_{2}.

This allows as to define
\Omega^{(k)}Q
as

(12.9)

\Omega^{(k)}Q=\Omega(i)

for any chosen embedding
i:Q\rightarrowtail{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}.
The homology of this complex is called
k
-power homology
H^{(k)}_{*}(Q)=H_{*}(\Omega^{(k)}Q).

12.2.
Definition of Grigor’yan-Muranov-Vershinin-Yau

Proposition 12.1
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative ring and
k\cdot 1_{\mathbb{K}}
is invertible in
\mathbb{K}.
Then the chain complex
\Omega^{(k)}Q
is isomorphic to the chain complex defined in
[
18
]
.

Proof.

Grigor’yan-Muranov-Vershinin-Yau define a graded module
\Lambda(Q)
for any quiver
Q
such that
\Lambda_{n}(Q)
consists of linear combinations of non-degenerated
n
-paths of
Q
. In other words
\Lambda_{n}(Q)
is the
n
th graded component of the path algebra
\mathbb{K}[Q].
For the case
Q={\sf Q}^{k}_{V}
they define a structure of chain complex on
\Lambda({\sf Q}^{k}_{V}).

For two edges
\alpha^{v^{\prime},v}_{m},\alpha^{v^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime}}_{n}
they set

(12.10)

[\alpha^{v^{\prime},v}_{m}\alpha^{v^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime}}_{n}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}^{v^{\prime\prime},v}.

It is easy to see that is it related to the composition in
\mathpzc{A}_{V}^{k}
by the formula

(12.11)

\alpha^{v^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime}}_{n}\circ\alpha^{v^{\prime},v}_{m}=\frac{1}{k}[\alpha^{v^{\prime},v}_{m}\alpha^{v^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime}}_{n}].

They define the maps
d_{i}:\Lambda_{n}({\sf Q}_{V}^{k})\to\Lambda_{n-1}({\sf Q}^{k}_{V})
by the formulas

(12.12)

\begin{split}d_{0}(a_{1}\dots a_{n})&=k\cdot a_{2}\dots a_{n},\\
d_{i}(a_{1}\dots a_{n})&=a_{1}{\dots}\cdot[\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}]\cdot\dots a_{n},\hskip 28.45274pt1\leq i\leq n-1,\\
d_{n}(a_{1}\dots a_{n})&=k\cdot a_{1}\dots a_{n-1},\end{split}

where
a_{i}\in({\sf Q}_{V}^{k})_{1},

and they define the differential
\partial_{n}=\sum(-1)^{i}d_{i}.
It is easy to see that the multiplication by
\frac{1}{k}
induces an isomorphism of complexes

(12.13)

\frac{1}{k}\cdot:\Lambda_{n}({\sf Q}^{k}_{V})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{N}({\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V})).

Further, for any embedding
i:Q\rightarrowtail{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}
they consider
\Lambda(Q)
as a graded submodule of the chain complex
\Lambda({\sf Q}_{V}^{k})
and set

(12.14)

\Omega^{(k)}Q=\omega(\Lambda({\sf Q}_{V}^{k}),\Lambda(Q)).

It is easy to see that the map (
12.13
) takes
\Lambda(Q)
to the image of

(12.15)

\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q]\to\mathsf{N}({\sf Lnerve}(\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V})).

The assertion follows.
∎

Remark 12.2
.

For the definition of
\Omega^{(k)}Q
in
[
18
]
it is not assumed that
k\cdot 1_{\mathbb{K}}
is invertible. However, it is assumed for the proof that the homology
H_{*}(\Omega^{(k)}Q)
is homotopy invariant
[
18
, Th.5.5]
.

12.3.
Functoriality, strong morphisms

We say that a morphism of quivers
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
is non-degenerate, if any non-degenerate edge maps to a non-degenerate edge
f(Q_{1}^{\sf N})\subseteq(Q^{\prime})_{1}^{\sf N}.
Otherwise it is degenerate. For example the projection
\mathsf{q}^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{q}^{0}
is degenerate as well as the homotopy of the identical map with itself
Q\square\mathsf{q}^{1}\twoheadrightarrow Q.
To compare, in
[
18
]
the definition of quivers without degenerate edges is used, and so only non-degenerate morphisms are considered there. Using the terminology of
[
18
]
, a non-degenerate morphism
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
is strong, if the map
Q(v,u)\to Q^{\prime}(f(v),f(u))
is injective for any
v,u\in Q_{0}.
So strong morphisms between quivers are analogues of faithful functors between categories.

The wide subcategory of
\mathsf{Quiv}
with strong morphisms is denoted by
{\sf SQuiv}.
The full subcategory of
{\sf SQuiv}
consisted of quivers of power at most
k
is denoted by

(12.16)

{\sf SQuiv}^{k}\subseteq{\sf SQuiv}\subseteq\mathsf{Quiv}.

It is easy to check that any strong morphism
f:{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}\to{\sf Q}^{k}_{U}
induces a linear functor
\mathpzc{A}(f):\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V}\to\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{U}
(if
f
is not strong,
\mathpzc{A}(f)
is not a functor, because
\mathpzc{A}(f)(z_{v^{\prime},v})\neq z_{f(v^{\prime}),v}
). On the other hand, it is easy to check that any strong morphism
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
of quivers of power at most
k
can be embedded into a diagram

(12.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf Q}^{k}_{V^{\prime}},}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\leftarrowtail
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\leftarrowtail
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}

where
\tilde{f}
is strong,
i,i^{\prime}
are embeddings and
V=Q_{0},V^{\prime}=Q^{\prime}_{0}.
This defines a morphism of marked linear categories

(12.18)

(\mathpzc{A}(\tilde{f}),f):\mathpzc{L}(i)\longrightarrow\mathpzc{L}(i^{\prime}).

By Proposition
3.5
, the induced morphism
\Omega^{(k)}Q\to\Omega^{(k)}Q^{\prime}
does not depend on the choice of
\tilde{f}
and we denote it by

(12.19)

\Omega^{(k)}f:\Omega^{(k)}Q\longrightarrow\Omega^{(k)}Q^{\prime}.

This defines a functor

(12.20)

\Omega^{(k)}:{\sf SQuiv}^{k}\longrightarrow{\sf Ch},

where
{\sf Ch}
is the category of chain complexes.

12.4.
Homotopy invariance of
k
-power homology

We can define a weak cylinder functor for
{\sf SQuiv}
as follows

(12.21)

{\sf cyl}=-\square\mathsf{q}^{1}:{\sf SQuiv}^{k}\longrightarrow{\sf SQuiv}^{k}.

We say that two strong morphisms of quivers

f,g:Q\to Q^{\prime}
are strongly
homotopic if they are homotopic with respect to this weak cylinder functor. Note that in this definition we assume that the homotopy
h:{\sf cyl}(Q)\to Q^{\prime}
is also a strong morphism.

Proposition 12.3

(cf.
[
18
, Th.5.5]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative ring and
k\geq 1
be a natural number such that
k\cdot 1_{\mathbb{K}}
is invertible in
\mathbb{K}
. Strongly homotopic strong morphisms
f\sim g:Q\to Q^{\prime}
of quivers of power at most
k
induce homotopic morphisms of complexes

(12.22)

\Omega^{(k)}f\sim\Omega^{(k)}g:\Omega^{(k)}Q\longrightarrow\Omega^{(k)}Q^{\prime}.

Proof.

We can assume that
f
and
g
are one-step homotopic. Let
h:Q\square\mathsf{q}^{1}\to Q^{\prime}
be a homotopy from
f
to
g.

Chose embeddings
i:Q\rightarrowtail{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}
and
i^{\prime}:Q^{\prime}\rightarrowtail{\sf Q}^{k}_{V^{\prime}},
where
V=Q_{0}
and
V^{\prime}=Q_{0}^{\prime},
and chose some strong morphisms
\tilde{f},\tilde{g}:{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}\to{\sf Q}^{k}_{V^{\prime}}
that extend
f
and
g.
They define linear functors
\mathpzc{A}(f),\mathpzc{A}(g):\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V}\to\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V^{\prime}}.
Consider a functor
H:\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{F}(\mathsf{q}^{1})]\to\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V^{\prime}}
which is defined on objects such that
H(v,0)=f(v)
and
H(v,1)=g(v)
and which is defined on morphisms such that

•

for any edge
\alpha\in{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}(v,u)
we have
H(\alpha\otimes{\sf id}_{0})=\tilde{f}(\alpha),

H(\alpha\otimes{\sf id}_{1})=\tilde{g}(\alpha);

•

H({\sf id}_{v}\otimes(0,1))=h(({\sf id}_{v},(0,1)));

•

for a non-degenerate edge
\alpha\in{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}(v,u)
we set
H(\alpha\otimes(0,1))=z_{g(u),f(v)}.

It is easy to check that this functor is well-defined and it defies a morphism of marked linear categories

(12.23)

H:\mathpzc{L}(i)\square I^{\sf lm}\longrightarrow\mathpzc{L}(i^{\prime})

that shows that the maps
(\mathpzc{A}(\tilde{f}),f),(\mathpzc{A}(\tilde{g}),g):\mathpzc{L}(i)\to\mathpzc{L}(i^{\prime})
are homotopic in the sense of marked linear categories. Then the assertion follows from (
11.12
).
∎

13.
Square-commutative homology of quivers

In this section we will present two equivalent (Proposition
13.4
) definitions for a square-commutative homology of quivers and prove their basic properties. The corresponding theory coincides with path homology for graphs without non-degenerate directed triangles and double edges (Theorem
13.12
).

Definition 13.1
.

For a quiver
Q
we denote by
\mathpzc{Z}(Q)
a category such that
{\sf ob}(\mathpzc{Z}(Q))=Q_{0}
and
\mathpzc{Z}(Q)(v,u)=Q(v,u)\cup\{z_{v,u}\},
where
z_{v,u}
is a new formal arrow. The composition is defined so that for any
non-degenerated
edges
\alpha:v\to v^{\prime}
and
\beta:v^{\prime}\to v^{\prime\prime}
we set
\beta\circ\alpha=z_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}.
Degenerated edges
s(v)=1_{v}
are the identity morphisms in this category.
Then we define the complex
\Omega^{\sf sc}Q
as the complex associated with the marked category
(\mathpzc{Z}(Q),Q_{1})

(13.1)

\Omega^{\sf sc}Q=\Omega(\mathpzc{Z}(Q),Q_{1}).

Note that the set
I=\{z_{v,u}\mid v,u\in Q_{0}\}
is an ideal of
\mathpzc{Z}(Q).
Hence, Proposition
10.12
implies that

(13.2)

\Omega^{\sf sc}_{n}Q=\{x\in(\mathsf{N}Q_{1})_{n}\mid\tilde{d}_{i}(x)=0,\ \text{for}\ 1\leq i\leq n-1\}.

This definition of square-commutative homology is not always convenient for proving its properties. So, we define another category
\mathpzc{C}(Q)
such that
Q\subseteq\mathpzc{C}(Q)
and prove that the corresponding complexes coincide.

Definition 13.2
.

By
\mathpzc{F}(Q)
we denote the path category (free category) of
Q.
Consider its quotient
\mathpzc{C}(Q)=\mathpzc{F}(Q)/\sim,
where
\sim
is the minimal congruence relation such that
\alpha\beta\sim\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}
for any
non-degenerate
arrows
\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in Q_{1}^{\sf N}
such that
t(\beta)=t(\alpha^{\prime}),h(\alpha)=h(\beta^{\prime}),h(\beta)=t(\alpha),h(\alpha^{\prime})=t(\beta^{\prime}).

(13.3)

\mathpzc{C}(Q)=\mathpzc{F}(Q)/\{\alpha\beta=\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\},\hskip 28.45274pt\hbox to71.83pt{\vbox to52.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 36.21469pt\lower-26.26639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{{{{}}{
