{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -11.56 -20.21 L 11.56 -20.21}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-8.3556pt}{-14.60417pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@discardpath@clipped{M 0 -5.96 h 5.36 v 11.92 h -5.36 Z} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.12506pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{57}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.3556pt}{-14.60417pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@discardpath@clipped{M 0 -5.96 h 5.36 v 11.92 h -5.36 Z} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.12506pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{57}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.61964pt}{-22.75253pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Such squares in
Q
will be called
non-degenerate directed squares
. So, roughly speaking, in
\mathpzc{C}(Q)
we make all non-degenerate squares commutative. In the category
\mathpzc{F}(Q)
there is a notion of the length of a morphism: the length of the path in the original quiver. We assume that the length of identity morphisms is zero. Since we take a quotient so that only morphisms of the same length are equivalent, the length of a morphism is well defined in
\mathpzc{C}(Q).

We denote by
{\sf NQuiv}
the category of quivers and non-degenerate morphisms. The constructions
\mathpzc{Z}(Q)
and
\mathpzc{C}(Q)
are natural with respect to non-degenerate morphisms and define functors to the category of small categories

(13.4)

\mathpzc{Z},\mathpzc{C}:{\sf NQuiv}\longrightarrow{\sf Cat}.

In particular, we obtain a functor

(13.5)

\Omega^{\sf sc}:{\sf NQuiv}\longrightarrow{\sf Ch}.

Remark 13.3
.

Note that the constructions
\mathpzc{Z},\mathpzc{C}
are not natural on the whole category of quivers
\mathsf{Quiv}
with not necessary non-degenerate morphisms. For example, they are not well-defined for the projection
\mathsf{q}^{2}\to\mathsf{q}^{0}.

For any quiver
Q
we consider a functor

(13.6)

\tau:\mathpzc{C}(Q)\to\mathpzc{Z}(Q)

which is identical on
Q
. It sends a path from
v
to
u
of length at least two to
z_{v,u}.
This functor is natural on
Q,
so we can say that it is a natural transformation
\tau:\mathpzc{C}\to\mathpzc{Z}.
This functor induces a morphism of marked categories

(13.7)

\tau:(\mathpzc{C}(Q),Q_{1})\longrightarrow(\mathpzc{Z}(Q),Q_{1}).

Proposition 13.4
.

For any commutative ring
\mathbb{K}
the morphism (
13.7
) induces a natural isomorphism

(13.8)

\Omega(\mathpzc{C}(Q),Q_{1})\cong\Omega^{\sf sc}Q.

Proof.

We denote by
Q
the quiver
Q
considered as a subquiver of
\mathpzc{C}(Q)
and we denote by
Q^{\prime}
the quiver
Q
considered as a subquiver of
\mathpzc{Z}(Q).
Then
Q_{1}^{2}
consists of all morphisms of length
\leq 2.
So there are two types of morphisms in
Q_{1}^{2}:
(1) morphisms from
Q_{1}
; (2) morphisms of length
2.
Similarly there are two types of morphisms in
(Q^{\prime}_{1})^{2}
: (1) morphisms from
Q^{\prime}_{1};
(2) morphisms
z_{v,u}
for such couples of
(v,u)
that there exists a path of length
2
from
v
to
u
in
Q^{\prime}_{1}
.
It is easy to see that
\tau
induces a bijection between all isomorphisms
Q_{1}\cong Q_{1}
and
Q_{1}^{2}\cong(Q^{\prime}_{1})^{2}.

Then the assertion follows from Proposition
10.10
.
∎

Remark 13.5
.

Note that the functor
\tau:\mathpzc{C}(Q)\to\mathpzc{Z}(Q)
generally is not surjective on morphisms. For example, if
Q=\mathsf{q}^{1},
then
\mathpzc{C}(\mathsf{q}^{1})
has only one non-identical morphism and
\mathpzc{Z}(\mathsf{q}^{1})
has two non-identical morphisms. The image
\mathpzc{Z}^{\prime}(Q)
of
\mathpzc{C}(Q)
in
\mathpzc{Z}(Q)
can be described as follows:

(13.9)

\mathpzc{Z}^{\prime}(Q)(v,u)=\begin{cases}Q(v,u)\cup\{z_{v,u}\},&\text{there is a path of length $\geq 2$ from $u$ to $v$}\\
Q(v,u),&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Since
Q\subseteq\mathpzc{Z}^{\prime}(Q)\subseteq\mathpzc{Z}(Q)
by Corollary
10.11
we obtain that

(13.10)

\Omega^{\sf sc}Q\cong\Omega(\mathpzc{Z}^{\prime}(Q),Q_{1}).

13.1.
Vanishing of square commutative homology

Proposition 13.6
.

If
Q
has no non-degenerated directed squares, then

(13.11)

\Omega_{n}^{\sf sc}Q=0,\hskip 28.45274pt\text{for}\ n\geq 2,

and
\Omega_{n}^{\sf sc}Q=\mathbb{K}^{Q_{n}}
for
n=0,1.
Moreover,
H^{\sf sc}_{n}(Q)
is isomorphic to the homology of the quiver treated as
1
-dimensional space.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
10.13
and the equation
\mathpzc{C}(Q)=\mathpzc{F}(Q)
for this kind of quivers.
∎

13.2.
Box product and square-commutative homology

Lemma 13.7
.

For any two quivers
Q,Q^{\prime}
there is an isomorphism

(13.12)

\mathpzc{F}(Q)\times\mathpzc{F}(Q^{\prime})\cong\mathpzc{F}(Q\square Q^{\prime})/\sim,

which is an identity on
Q\square Q^{\prime},
where
\sim
is the minimal congruence relation such that
(\alpha,1_{u^{\prime}})(1_{v},\beta)\sim(1_{u},\beta)(\alpha,1_{v^{\prime}})
for any
\alpha\in Q(v,u)
and
\beta\in Q(v^{\prime},u^{\prime}).

Proof.

The inclusion
Q\square Q^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{F}(Q)\times\mathpzc{F}(Q^{\prime})
defines a full functor
\mathpzc{F}(Q\square Q^{\prime})\to\mathpzc{F}(Q)\times\mathpzc{F}(Q^{\prime}),
which is an identity on objects. This functor sends both
(\alpha,{\sf id}_{u^{\prime}})({\sf id}_{v},\beta)
and
({\sf id}_{u},\beta)(\alpha,{\sf id}_{v^{\prime}})
to
(\alpha,\beta).
Hence, we have a full functor
\mathpzc{F}(Q\square Q^{\prime})/\sim\ \to\mathpzc{F}(Q)\times\mathpzc{F}(Q^{\prime}).

The relation
(\alpha,{\sf id}_{u^{\prime}})({\sf id}_{v},\beta)\sim({\sf id}_{u},\beta)(\alpha,{\sf id}_{v^{\prime}})
allows to present any morphism of
\mathpzc{F}(Q\square Q^{\prime})/\sim
in the form
(\alpha_{1},1)(\alpha_{2},1)\dots(\alpha_{n},1)(1,\beta_{1})(1,\beta_{2})\dots(1,\beta_{m}).
It follows that the functor is injective on hom-sets. Hence the functor is an isomorphism.
∎

Proposition 13.8
.

There is a functor

(13.13)

\mathpzc{C}(Q)\times\mathpzc{C}(Q^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathpzc{C}(Q\square Q^{\prime})

which is identical on
Q\square Q^{\prime}.
Moreover, if
Q=G
and
Q=G^{\prime}
are directed graphs, then this is an isomorphism

(13.14)

\mathpzc{C}(G)\times\mathpzc{C}(G^{\prime})\cong\mathpzc{C}(G\square G^{\prime}).

Proof.

By the definition, we have a functor
\mathpzc{F}(Q\square Q^{\prime})\to\mathpzc{C}(Q\square Q^{\prime}).
The compositions
(\alpha,1_{u^{\prime}})(1_{v},\beta)
and
(1_{u},\beta)(\alpha,1_{v^{\prime}})
are mapped to to the same morphism. By Lemma
13.7
we obtain a functor
\mathpzc{F}(Q)\times\mathpzc{F}(Q^{\prime})\to\mathpzc{C}(Q\square Q^{\prime})
which obviously induces a functor
\mathpzc{C}(Q)\times\mathpzc{C}(Q^{\prime})\to\mathpzc{C}(Q\square Q^{\prime}).

Now assume that
Q=G
and
Q^{\prime}=G^{\prime}
are digraphs. In this case there are three types of non-degenerate directed squares in
G\square G^{\prime}:
(1) a non-degenerate square in
G
times an object of
G^{\prime}
; (2) an object of
G
times a non-degenerate square in
G
; (3) squares of the type

(13.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(v,v^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u,v^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(v,u^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u,u^{\prime}).}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha,1)}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,\beta)}^{\prime}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,\beta)}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha,1)}

Here we use that there is at most one arrow
v\to u
and at most one arrow
v^{\prime}\to u^{\prime}.
All these relations, corresponding these three types of squares, are satisfied in
\mathpzc{C}(G)\times\mathpzc{C}(G^{\prime}).
Hence, there is a functor
\mathpzc{C}(G\square G^{\prime})\to\mathpzc{C}(G)\times\mathpzc{C}(G^{\prime}),
which is identical on
G\square G^{\prime}.
Since
G\square G^{\prime}
is a generating set of morphisms in both categories, and both functors
\mathpzc{C}(G\square G^{\prime})\to\mathpzc{C}(G)\times\mathpzc{C}(G^{\prime})
and
\mathpzc{C}(G)\times\mathpzc{C}(G^{\prime})\to\mathpzc{C}(G\square G^{\prime})
are identical on
G\square G^{\prime},
the compositions
\mathpzc{C}(G)\times\mathpzc{C}(G^{\prime})\to\mathpzc{C}(G\square G^{\prime})\to\mathpzc{C}(G)\times\mathpzc{C}(G^{\prime})
and
\mathpzc{C}(G\square G^{\prime})\to\mathpzc{C}(G)\times\mathpzc{C}(G^{\prime})\to\mathpzc{C}(G)\times\mathpzc{C}(G^{\prime})
are also identical, the functors are isomorphisms.
∎

Proposition 13.9
.

If
\mathbb{K}
is a principal ideal domain and
G,G^{\prime}
are directed graphs, then there is an isomorphism of complexes

(13.16)

\Omega^{\sf sc}(G\square G^{\prime})\cong\Omega^{\sf sc}G\otimes\Omega^{\sf sc}G^{\prime}.

Moreover, there is a short exact sequence

(13.17)

0\to\bigoplus_{i+j=n}H^{\sf sc}_{i}(G)\otimes H^{\sf sc}_{j}(G^{\prime})\to H^{\sf sc}_{n}(G\square G^{\prime})\to\bigoplus_{i+j=n-1}{\sf Tor}^{\mathbb{K}}_{1}(H^{\sf sc}_{i}(G),H^{\sf sc}_{j}(G^{\prime}))\to 0.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
13.8
and Proposition
10.7
.
∎

Remark 13.10
.

Proposition
13.9
can’t be generalised to the case of all quivers (see Example
13.21
).

13.3.
Homotopy invariance of square-commutative homology

As usual we define a weak cylinder functor
{\sf cyl}=-\square\mathsf{q}^{1}:{\sf NQuiv}\to{\sf NQuiv}
and define non-degenerately homotopic non-degenerate morphisms of quivers via this weak cylinder functor.

Proposition 13.11
.

For any commutative ring
\mathbb{K}
two non-degenerately homotopic non-degenerate morphisms of quivers
f\sim g:Q\to Q^{\prime}
induce homotopic morphisms of chain complexes

(13.18)

\Omega^{\sf sc}f\sim\Omega^{\sf sc}g:\Omega^{\sf sc}Q\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sf sc}Q^{\prime}.

Proof.

We denote by
F:{\sf NQuiv}\to{\sf MarkCat}
the functor given by
F(Q)=(\mathpzc{C}(Q),Q_{1}).
Using that
\mathpzc{C}(\mathsf{q}^{1})=\mathpzc{F}(\mathsf{q}^{1})
we obtain that
{\sf cyl}(F(Q))=(\mathpzc{C}(Q)\times\mathpzc{C}(\mathsf{q}^{1}),(Q\square\mathsf{q}^{1})_{1})
and
F({\sf cyl}(Q))=(\mathpzc{C}(Q\square\mathsf{q}^{1}),(Q\square\mathsf{q}^{1})_{1}).

Proposition
13.8
implies that there is a natural transformation

{\sf cyl}\>F\to F\>{\sf cyl}.

Then the assertion follows from Proposition
2.1
and Proposition
10.9
.
∎

13.4.
Comparison of square-commutative and path homology

For a set
V
we denote by
{\sf c}(V)
the category whose set of objects is
V
and each hom-set is one-element
{\sf c}(V)(v,u)=\{(v,u)\}
. Recall that the path homology of a digraph
G
are defined as homology of the marked category
({\sf c}(V),G),
where
V=G_{0}
is the vertex set of
G.
There is a unique functor
\mathpzc{Z}(G)\to{\sf c}(V)
which is identical on objects. It sends a morphism
\alpha:v\to u
to the morphism
(v,u):v\to u.
It is easy to see that this defines a morphism of marked categories

(\mathpzc{Z}(G),G)\longrightarrow({\sf c}(V),G),

which defines a morphism of chain complexes

(13.19)

\Omega^{\sf sc}G\longrightarrow\Omega G.

A non-degenerated directed triangle of a quiver
Q
is a triple of non-degenerated arrows
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
such that
t(\alpha)=t(\gamma),h(\alpha)=t(\beta),h(\beta)=h(\gamma)

(13.20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

A double edge in a digraph
G=(V,E)
is a is a two element set of vertices
\{u,v\}
such that
(u,v),(v,u)\in E.

Theorem 13.12
.

Let
G
be a digraph without non-degenerate directed triangles and double edges. Then the morphism (
13.19
) is an isomorphism

(13.21)

\Omega^{\sf sc}G\cong\Omega G.

Proof.

We denote by
M
the arrows of the digraph
G
considered as morphisms of
\mathpzc{Z}(G)
and denote by
M^{\prime}
the arrows of digraph
G

considered as morphisms of
{\sf c}(V).
The set
M^{2}
is a disjoint union of two sets of morphisms: (1) arrows of
M
; (2) morphisms
z_{v,u}
for such couples that there is a path of length
2
from
v
to
u.
The set
(M^{\prime})^{2}
is also a
disjoint
union of two sets: (1) edges
(v,u)\in E
(2) couples
(v,u)
such that there is a path of length
2
from
v
to
u.
The fact that the last two sets are disjoint follows from the fact that
G
has no non-degenerate directed triangles and double edges. Then it is easy to see that the morphism
(\mathpzc{Z}(G),M)\to({\sf c}(V),M^{\prime})
induces isomorphisms
M\cong M^{\prime}
and
M^{2}\cong(M^{\prime})^{2}.
Then the assertion follows from Proposition
10.10
.
∎

13.5.
Simplicial complexes and square-commutative homology

Following
[
14
]
we can associate two graphs
G(S)
and
G^{\prime}(S)
with a simplicial complex
S
, such that

(13.22)

G(S)\subseteq G^{\prime}(S).

The vertices of the graph
G^{\prime}(S)
are simplices of
S.
For two simplices
\sigma,\tau\in S
there is an arrow
\sigma\to\tau
in
G^{\prime}(S)
if and only if
\tau\subseteq\sigma.
The graph
G(S)
is a subgraph of
G^{\prime}(S)
with the same set of vertices. The arrow
\sigma\to\tau
is in
G(S)
if and only if
\tau\subseteq\sigma
and
{\sf dim}(\sigma)={\sf dim}(\tau)+1.
It is proved in
[
14
]
that

(13.23)

H_{*}(S)\cong\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G(S))\cong\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G^{\prime}(S)).

Theorem 13.13
.

For any simplicial complex
S
there is an isomorphism

(13.24)

H_{*}(S)\cong H^{\sf sc}_{*}(G(S)).

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
13.12
, the fact that there are no non-degenerate directed triangles and double edges in
G(S),
and the isomorphism (
13.23
).
∎

13.6.
Comparison of square-commutative and
k
-power homology

Let
k\geq 1
be an integer such that
k\cdot 1_{\mathbb{K}}
is invertible in
\mathbb{K}
and let
Q
be a quiver of power at most
k.
Consider an embedding
i:Q\rightarrowtail{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}
and the corresponding embedding
i{A}:Q\rightarrowtail\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V}
(see Subsection
12.1
). Consider a functor
i{Z}:\mathpzc{Z}(Q)\to\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V}
which sends
\alpha\in Q_{1}
to
i(\alpha)
and
z_{v,v^{\prime}}
to
\zeta_{v,v^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}^{v,v^{\prime}}.
It is easy to see that this is a functor which induces a morphism of marked linear categories

(13.25)

i{A}:(\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{Z}(Q)],Q)\longrightarrow(\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V},i{A}(Q)).

This induces a morphism of chain complexes

(13.26)

\Omega^{\sf sc}Q\longrightarrow\Omega^{(k)}Q.

Proposition 13.14
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative ring such that
k\cdot 1_{\mathbb{K}}
is invertible in
\mathbb{K}
and let
Q
be a quiver of power at most
k-1.
Then the morphism (
13.26
) is an isomorphism

(13.27)

\Omega^{\sf sc}Q\cong\Omega^{(k)}Q.

Proof.

Since the power of
Q
is less then
k,
we have
i(Q)(v,u)\subsetneqq{\sf Q}^{k}_{V}(v,u)
for each
v,u\in V.
It follows that the set
i{A}(Q)(v,u)\cup\{\zeta_{v,u}\}
is linearly independent in
\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V}(v,u)
. Therefore
i{Z}:\mathbb{K}[\mathpzc{Z}(Q)]\to\mathpzc{A}^{k}_{V}
is an embedding. Then the assertion follows from Remark
3.1
.
∎

Corollary 13.15

(cf.
[
18
, Th.4.4]
)
.

Let
k,

l
be positive integers such that
k\cdot 1_{\mathbb{K}}
and
l\cdot 1_{\mathbb{K}}
are invertible in
\mathbb{K},
and let
Q
be a quiver of power strictly less that
k
and
l.
Then there are isomorphisms

(13.28)

\Omega^{(k)}Q\cong\Omega^{(l)}Q,\hskip 28.45274ptH^{(k)}_{*}(Q)\cong H^{(l)}_{*}(Q).

Remark 13.16
.

Let
k,l
are two distinct positive integers and
Q
is a quiver whose power is strictly less than
k
and
l.
On one hand, in

[
18
, Remark 4.5]
it is stated that
H_{*}^{(k)}(Q)
and
H_{*}^{(l)}(Q)
are not necessarily isomorphic in this case. They state that there is an isomorphism of modules
\Omega^{(k)}_{n}Q\cong\Omega^{(l)}_{n}Q,
which is not compatible with the differential. On the other hand, Corollary
13.15
states that the complexes
\Omega^{(k)}Q
and
\Omega^{(l)}Q
are isomorphic as well as their homology
H_{*}^{(k)}(Q)
and
H_{*}^{(l)}(Q)
. This is because in
[
18
]
authors use another approach to the definition that allows to define
k
-power homology without the assumption that
k\cdot 1_{\mathbb{K}}
is invertible. But our approach to the definition (that uses marked linear categories) allows us to define the
k
-power homology only assuming that
k\cdot 1_{\mathbb{K}}
is invertible. So, the modules
H_{*}^{(k)}(Q)
and
H_{*}^{(l)}(Q)
can be non-isomorphic only if at least one of the integers
k
and
l
is not invertible in
\mathbb{K}
. The authors show that in the case of
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{Z}
the groups
H^{(k)}_{0}(Q,\mathbb{Z})
and
H^{(l)}_{0}(Q,\mathbb{Z})
are non-isomorphic
[
18
, Prop 4.3]
.

13.7.
Examples

Example 13.17

(cf.
[
18
, Example 4.6]
)
.

Let
G
be a digraph with three vertices and there non-degenerate arrows

(13.29)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

By Proposition
13.6
we have
H_{0}^{\sf sc}(G)=H_{1}^{\sf sc}(G)\cong\mathbb{K}
and
H_{n}^{\sf sc}(G)=0
for
n\geq 2.
On the other hand this graph is contractible in the sense of GLMY-theory
[
12
]
, and hence

(13.30)

H_{1}^{\sf sc}(G)\not\cong\mathsf{PH}_{1}(G)=0.

Note that we also have a variant of homotopy invariance theorem for square-commutative homology (Proposition
13.11
) but it works only for
non-degenerately
homotopic
non-degenerated
morphisms. This weak version of homotopy invariance theorem does not allow to prove that graphs contractible in the sense of GLMY-theory have trivial square-commutative homology.

Example 13.18
.

Take the graph
G
from the previous example (
13.29
) and assume that
\mathbb{K}
is a principal ideal domain. Then by Proposition
13.9
we have

(13.31)

H_{i}^{\sf sc}(G^{\square n})=\mathbb{K}^{\binom{n}{i}},

where
G^{\square n}=G\square\dots\square G.

Example 13.19
.

A large source of examples is Theorem
13.13
. For instance, we can take a triangulation
S
of the Klein bottle and obtain a digraph
G(S)
such that
H^{\sf sc}_{1}(G(S),\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2.
This example shows that there can be torsion in
H^{\sf sc}_{*}(G,\mathbb{Z}).

Example 13.20
.

Consider the quiver
Q:

(13.32)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{2}}

and the category
\mathpzc{Z}^{\prime}(Q)
(see Remark
13.5
). There are four non-degenerate composable
2
-sequences
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{j})
for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
that form a basis of
\mathsf{N}_{2}\mathpzc{Z}^{\prime}(Q)
and
\mathsf{N}_{n}\mathpzc{Z}^{\prime}(Q)=0
for
n\geq 1.
By (
13.2
) we have
\Omega^{\sf sc}_{2}(Q)={\sf Ker}(\tilde{d}_{1}:\overline{\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q]}_{2}\to\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{Z}^{\prime}(Q)_{1})
and
\Omega_{n}^{\sf sc}(Q)=\overline{\mathbb{K}[{\sf P}Q]}_{n}
for
n=0,1.
For an element
x=a_{11}(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})+a_{12}(\alpha_{1},\beta_{2})+a_{21}(\alpha_{2},\beta_{1})+a_{22}(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2})\in(\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{Z}^{\prime}(Q))_{2}
the condition
d_{1}(x)=0
is equivalent to
a_{11}+a_{12}+a_{21}+a_{22}=0.
Therefore we have a 3-element basis of
\Omega^{\sf sc}_{2}(Q)
given by
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})-(\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}),

(\alpha_{1},\beta_{2})-(\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}),
and
(\alpha_{2},\beta_{1})-(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}).

(13.33)

\Omega^{\sf sc}Q:\hskip 28.45274pt0\to\mathbb{K}^{3}\to\mathbb{K}^{4}\to\mathbb{K}^{3}.

Simple computation shows that that homology of this complex is

(13.34)

H_{0}^{\sf sc}(Q)=\mathbb{K},\hskip 14.22636ptH_{1}^{\sf sc}(Q)=0,\hskip 14.22636ptH_{2}^{\sf sc}(Q)=\mathbb{K}

and the non-trivial
2
-cycle is given by
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})-(\alpha_{1},\beta_{2})-(\alpha_{2},\beta_{1})+(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}).

Example 13.21
.

Consider the quiver with one loop and the quiver with two loops.

Note that

Q^{(2)}=Q^{(1)}\square Q^{(1)}.

It is easy to compute that
\Omega^{\sf sc}(Q^{(1)})
has the following form

(13.36)

\Omega^{\sf sc}(Q^{(1)}):\hskip 28.45274pt0\to\mathbb{K}\overset{0}{\to}\mathbb{K}

and the square commutative homology are equal to homology of the circle.

(13.37)

H^{\sf sc}_{*}(Q^{(1)})=H_{*}(S^{1}).

In particular
\Omega^{\sf sc}_{n}(Q^{(1)})=0
for
n\geq 2.
On the other hand, we claim that

(13.38)

\Omega^{\sf sc}_{n}(Q^{(2)})\neq 0,\hskip 28.45274ptn\geq 0.

Let us prove it. We denote by
W_{n}
the set of all sequences
s=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}),
where
\gamma_{i}\in\{\alpha,\beta\}.
We set
{\sf sgn}(s)=(-1)^{k},
where
k
the number of
\beta
in the sequence. Then it is easy to check that
\sum_{s\in W_{n}}{\sf sgn}(s)\langle s\rangle\in\Omega_{n}^{\sf sc}(Q^{(2)}).

This follows that

(13.39)

\Omega^{\sf sc}(Q^{(1)}\square Q^{(1)})\not\cong\Omega^{\sf sc}(Q^{(1)})\otimes\Omega^{\sf sc}(Q^{(1)}).

Hence, Proposition
13.9
can’t be generalised to all quivers.

14.
Marked groups

14.1.
Definition and basic properties

A subset of a group
M\subseteq G
is called pointed, if it contains the unit.
A marked group is a couple
(G,M),
where
G
is a group and
M
is a pointed subset of
G.
A group can be treated as a category with one object, so a marked group is a particular case of a marked category. Therefore a marked group
(G,M)

defines a chain complex
\Omega(G,M)
and its homology

(14.1)

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M):=H_{*}(\Omega(G,M))

is called path homology of the marked group. If we want to specify the ring
\mathbb{K}
we use the notation
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M,\mathbb{K}).
Note that if
M
is a subgroup of
G,
then
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)=H_{*}(M)
is just the ordinary group homology.

A morphism marked groups
f:(G,M)\to(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})
is a homomorphism
f:G\to G^{\prime}
such that
f(M)\subseteq M^{\prime}.
Such a morphism defines a morphism of complexes
\Omega(G,M)\to\Omega(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})
and a morphism of modules
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)\to\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G^{\prime},M^{\prime}).

14.1.1.
Conjugated homomorphisms

A “homotopy invariance” for marked groups has the following form. Let
f,g:(G,M)\to(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})
be two morphisms of marked groups such that there exists an element
m\in M^{\prime}
such that the homomorphisms are conjugated by this element i.e.

(14.2)

f(y)=m^{-1}g(y)m\hskip 28.45274pt\text{for any }y\in G.

Then
m
can be treated as a natural transformation from
f
to
g
if we treat them as functors between categories
G
and
G^{\prime}.
Then Proposition
10.8
and Proposition
10.9
imply that the induced morphisms of chain complexes are homotopic

(14.3)

\Omega f\sim\Omega g:\Omega(G,M)\longrightarrow\Omega(G^{\prime},M^{\prime}).

In particular, the homomorphisms
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(f)=\mathsf{PH}_{*}(g):\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)\to\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})
are equal.

14.1.2.
Isomorphism lemma for marked groups

For a subset
M\subseteq G
we set
M^{2}=\{mm^{\prime}\mid m,m^{\prime}\in M\}.
If
f:(G,M)\to(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})
is a morphism of marked groups such that
f
induces bijections
M\cong M^{\prime}
and
M^{2}\cong(M^{\prime})^{2},
then Proposition
10.10
implies that
f
induces an isomorphism

(14.4)

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)\cong\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G^{\prime},M^{\prime}).

In particular, if
G\subseteq G^{\prime}
then

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)\cong\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G^{\prime},M).

14.1.3.
Eilenberg–Zilber theorem and the Künneth formula

If
\mathbb{K}
is a principal ideal domain and
(G,M)
and
(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})
are marked groups, then Proposition
10.7
implies that there is an isomorphism of complexes

(14.5)

\Omega(G,M)\otimes\Omega(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})\cong\Omega(G\times G^{\prime},M\vee M^{\prime}),

where
M\vee M^{\prime}=(M\times 1)\cup(1\times M^{\prime}),

which implies that there is the corresponding Künneth-like short exact sequence: if we set
H_{*}=\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M),

H^{\prime}_{*}={\sf PH}_{*}(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})
and
H^{\prime\prime}_{*}={\sf PH}_{*}(G\times G^{\prime},M\vee M^{\prime})
then there is a natural short exact sequence.

(14.6)

0\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i+j=n}H_{i}\otimes H^{\prime}_{j}\longrightarrow H^{\prime\prime}_{n}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i+j=n-1}{\sf Tor}^{\mathbb{K}}_{1}(H_{i},H^{\prime}_{j})\longrightarrow 0.

14.1.4.
Free product

For two groups
G
and
G^{\prime}
we denote by
G*G^{\prime}
their free product and we denote by
\iota:G\to G*G^{\prime}
and
\iota^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\to G*G^{\prime}
the canonical embeddings.

Note that for any group
G
we have
\Omega_{0}G=\mathsf{N}_{0}G\cong\mathbb{K}
is generated by one element, which is the only object of
G,
treated a category. We set

(14.7)

R:={\sf Ker}(\mathsf{N}_{0}G\oplus\mathsf{N}_{0}G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{N}_{0}(G*G^{\prime}))\cong\mathbb{K}.

Proposition 14.1
.

For any marked groups
(G,M)
and
(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})
the morphisms
\iota,\iota^{\prime}
induce an isomorphism

(14.8)

\Omega(G*G^{\prime},\iota(M)\cup\iota^{\prime}(M^{\prime}))\cong(\Omega(G,M)\oplus\Omega(G^{\prime},M^{\prime}))/R.

In particular, we have an isomorphism

(14.9)

\mathsf{PH}_{n}(G*G^{\prime},\iota(M)\cup\iota^{\prime}(M^{\prime}))\cong\mathsf{PH}_{n}(G,M)\oplus\mathsf{PH}_{n}(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})

for
n\geq 1.

Proof.

For a group
\mathpzc{G}
we set
\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{G}=\mathsf{N}(\mathbb{K}[\mathsf{Nrv}(\mathpzc{G})]).
It is easy to see that the morphism
(\mathsf{N}G\oplus\mathsf{N}G^{\prime})/R\to\mathsf{N}(G*G^{\prime})
is a monomorphism. Moreover, this monomorphism induces an isomorphism of graded submodules
(\mathsf{N}M\oplus\mathsf{N}M^{\prime})/R\cong\mathsf{N}(\iota(M)\cup\iota^{\prime}(M^{\prime})).
Then the assertion follows from Remark
3.1
.
∎

Proposition 14.2
.

Let
G
be a group and
M,N
be subsets of
G,
containing unit, such that the sets
M^{2}\setminus 1,MN\setminus 1,NM\setminus 1,N^{2}\setminus 1
are disjoint and assume that for any
x_{1},x_{2}\in M
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in N
such that
(x_{1},y_{1})\neq(x_{2},y_{2})
we have
x_{1}y_{1}\neq x_{2}y_{2}
and
y_{1}x_{1}\neq y_{2}x_{2}.
Then for any
n\geq 1

(14.10)

\mathsf{PH}_{n}(G,M\cup N)\cong\mathsf{PH}_{n}(G,M)\oplus\mathsf{PH}_{n}(G,N).

Proof.

Consider the morphism of marked groups
(G*G,\iota(M)\cup\iota(N))\to(G,M\cup N).
The assumptions imply that this morphism induces bijections
\iota(M)\cup\iota(N)\cong M\cup N
and
(\iota(M)\cup\iota(N))^{2}\cong(M\cup N)^{2}.
Then the assertion follows from Proposition
14.1
and the isomorphism lemma (Paragraph
14.1.2
).
∎

Example 14.3
.

Assume that
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{Z}.
Take

(14.11)

G=\langle x,y\mid x^{2}=y^{2}=[[x,y],y]=1\rangle.

Then the subsets
\{1,x\}
and
\{1,y\}
are subgroups and
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,\{1,x\})\cong\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,\{1,y\})
are isomorphic to the homology of the two-element group. Therefore
Proposition
14.2
implies that

(14.12)

\mathsf{PH}_{n}(G,\{1,x,y\})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z},&n=0\\
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2},&n\text{ is odd}\\
0,&n\geq 2\text{ is even}\end{cases}

14.1.5.
Low dimensional homology

Proposotion
10.2
implies that
\Omega_{0}(G,M)=\mathbb{K},

\Omega_{1}(G,M)=\mathbb{K}[X]
and

(14.13)

\Omega_{2}(G,M)={\sf span}\{\langle x_{1},y_{1}\rangle-\langle x_{2},y_{2}\rangle\mid y_{1}x_{1}=y_{2}x_{2},x_{i},y_{i}\in X\}.

The differential
\Omega_{1}(G,M)\to\Omega_{0}(G,M)
is trivial. Hence, we have
\mathsf{PH}_{0}(G,M)=\mathbb{K}
and

(14.14)

\mathsf{PH}_{1}(G,M)=\mathbb{K}[X]/{\sf span}\{x_{1}+y_{1}-x_{2}-y_{2}\mid y_{1}x_{1}=y_{2}x_{2},x_{i},y_{i}\in M\}.

14.1.6.
Cohomology of marked groups, and their ideals

Since a marked group
(G,M)
can be treated as a marked category, following Subsection
10.4
we can consider the cohomology
\mathsf{PH}^{*}(G,M)
as a graded algebra. The construction is natural by
(G,M),
and for any morphism
f:(G,M)\to(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})
we obtain a homomorphism

(14.15)

\mathsf{PH}^{*}(G^{\prime},M^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathsf{PH}^{*}(G,M).

In particular, for any marked group
(G,M)
we have a homomorphism
H^{*}(G)\to\mathsf{PH}^{*}(G,M),
whose kernel is an ideal. Hence
M
defines an ideal

I_{M}\triangleleft H^{*}(G).

14.2.
Abelian groups and Pontryagin product

If
G
is an abelian group, then the product map
G\times G\to G
is a homomorphism. Using the Eilenberg-Zilber morphism
H_{*}(G)\otimes H_{*}(G)\to H_{*}(G\times G),
this allows us to define a map

(14.16)

H_{*}(G)\otimes H_{*}(G)\to H_{*}(G\times G)\to H_{*}(G),

which is called Pontryagin product on
H_{*}(G)
that makes
H_{*}(G)
graded-commutative algebra (
[
3
, Ch. V §5]
).

In the similar manner a marked abelian group
(G,M)
there is a morphism

(14.17)

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)\otimes\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)\to\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G\times G,M\vee M)\to\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M),

where the map
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)\otimes\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)\to\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G\times G,M\vee M)
is induced by the Eilenberg-Zilber map (see (
14.6
)). This product will be also called Pontryagin product on
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M).

Proposition 14.4
.

For a marked abelian group
(G,M)
the Pontryagin product (
14.17
) defines a structure of graded-commutative (assotiative, unital) algebra on
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)
that depends naturally of
(G,M).
Moreover, if
M=G,
then the Pontryagin product (
14.17
) coincides with the classical Pontryagin product on
H_{*}(G).

Proof.

It is well known
[
23
, p.220]
and can be easily checked that the Eilenberg-Zilber map is associative and commutative in the following sense. For any simplicial modules
A,A^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime}
the diagrams

(14.18)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}A\otimes\mathsf{N}A^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{N}A^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}(A\otimes A^{\prime})\otimes\mathsf{N}A^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}A\otimes\mathsf{N}(A^{\prime}\otimes A^{\prime\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}(A\otimes A^{\prime}\otimes A^{\prime\prime})}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon\otimes 1}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\varepsilon}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

and

(14.19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}A\otimes\mathsf{N}A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}(A\otimes A^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}A^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{N}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}(A^{\prime}\otimes A)}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}(T)}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

are commutative, where
t:\mathsf{N}A\otimes\mathsf{N}A^{\prime}\to\mathsf{N}A^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{N}A
is a morphism of complexes given by
t(a\otimes a^{\prime})=(-1)^{nn^{\prime}}a^{\prime}\otimes a
for
a\in\mathsf{N}_{n}A
and
a^{\prime}\in\mathsf{N}_{n^{\prime}}A^{\prime},
and
T:A\otimes A^{\prime}\to A^{\prime}\otimes A
is given by
T(a\otimes a^{\prime})=a^{\prime}\otimes a.
(Someone, who likes abstract nonsense, can say that
(\mathsf{N},\varepsilon)
is a symmetric lax monoidal functor
{\sf sMod}\to{\sf Ch}_{\geq 0}
. It is also related to “monoidal Dold-Kan correspondence”).
This follows that for any commutative simplicial algebra
\mathpzc{A}
there is a structure of graded-commutative algebra on
\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{A}
defined by the map
\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{A}\otimes\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{A}\overset{\varepsilon}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{N}(\mathpzc{A}\otimes\mathpzc{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{N}\mathpzc{A},
where
\mathpzc{A}\otimes\mathpzc{A}\to\mathpzc{A}
is the multiplication morphism.

In our case we consider the commutative simplicial algebra
\mathpzc{A}=\mathbb{K}[\mathsf{Nrv}(G)]
with the multiplication defined by multiplication on
G.
Then we obtain a structure of graded-commutative dg-algebra on
\mathsf{N}G:=\mathsf{N}(\mathbb{K}[\mathsf{Nrv}(G)])
that induces the Pontryagin product on
H_{*}(G)=H_{*}(\mathsf{N}G).
Lemma
8.8
implies that
\Omega(G,M)
is a dg-subalgebra of
\mathsf{N}G
and the following diagram is commutative.

(14.20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega(G,M)\otimes\Omega(G,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega(G\times G,X\vee X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega(G,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}G\otimes\mathsf{N}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}(G\times G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}G}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\hookleftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega(\mu)}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{N}(\mu)}

It follows that the Pontryagin product on
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)
is induced by the product on the subalgebra
\Omega(G,M)\subseteq\mathsf{N}G,
which is graded-commutative dg-algebra. It follows that
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)
with Pontryagin product is also a graded-commutative algebra. If
M=G,
then
\Omega(G,M)=\mathsf{N}G
and hence the Pontryagin product on
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,G)
coincides with the classical Pontryagin product.
∎

Remark 14.5
.

Proposition
14.4
implies that for any pointed subset of an abelian group
M\subseteq G,
the map
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)\to H_{*}(G)
is an algebra homomorphism. In particular, the image of this homomorphism is a subalgebra. So any
M\subseteq G
defines a subalgebra of
H_{*}(G).

14.3.
Coacyclic subsets

In this subsection we assume that
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{Z}.
A pointed subset
M\subseteq G
will be called coacyclic if the morphism
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M)\to H_{*}(G)
is an isomorphism. Further we list some properties of coacyclic subsets.

Proposition 14.6
.

If
M\subseteq G,M^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
are coacyclic subsets, then

(14.21)

M\vee M^{\prime}\subseteq G\times G^{\prime},\hskip 28.45274pt\iota(M)\cup\iota^{\prime}(M^{\prime})\subseteq G*G^{\prime}

are coacyclic subsets.

Proof.

It follows from (
14.6
) and Proposition
14.1
.
∎

Example 14.7
.

For any group
G
the group itself
X=G\subseteq G
is a coacyclic subset.

Example 14.8
.

By Proposition
14.6
for any groups
G,G^{\prime}

the subsets
G\vee G^{\prime}\subseteq G\times G
and
\iota(G)\cup\iota^{\prime}(G)\subseteq G*G^{\prime}
are coacyclic.

Example 14.9
.

It is easy to check that
\{0,1\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
is a coacyclic subset.

Example 14.10
.

Proposition
14.6
and Example
14.9
imply that
\{0,e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is a coacyclic subset, where
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Example 14.11
.

Proposition
14.6
and Example
14.9
imply that
\{1,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subseteq F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is a coacyclic subset, where
F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is a free group.

Example 14.12
.

For example, if we consider the Higman group

(14.22)

G=\langle x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\mid x_{i}^{-1}x_{i+1}x_{i}=x_{i+1}^{2},\ i\in\mathbb{Z}/4\rangle,

then
H_{n}(G)=0
for
n\geq 1
and the one-element set
M=\{1\}
is coacyclic in this group. More generally, one element set
M=\{1\}
is coacyclic in a group
G
if and only if
G
is acyclic.

Example 14.13
.

Here we present a non-example of coacyclic subset. Let
F=F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be a free group and
\gamma_{i}\subseteq F
be the lower central series of
F,
which is defined by the formula
\gamma_{i+1}=[\gamma_{i},F],
where
\gamma_{1}=F.
Take
i\geq 3,
set
G=F/\gamma_{i}
and let
M\subseteq G
to be the image of
\{1,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}.

Then the Hopf’s formula says that
H_{2}(G)=\gamma_{i}/\gamma_{i+1}\neq 0.
The equation (
14.4
) implies that
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(F,\{1,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\})=\mathsf{PH}_{*}(G,M).
Therefore
\mathsf{PH}_{2}(G,M)=0
and the generating set
M\subseteq G
is not coacyclic.

15.
Marked algebras

15.1.
Path Hochschild homology

In this section for simplicity we will assume that
\mathbb{K}
is a field. Let
\Lambda
be an (associative, unital)
\mathbb{K}
-algebra and
V
be a
\Lambda
-bimodule. We denote by
A(\Lambda,V)
the simplicial module such that
A(\Lambda)_{n}=V\otimes\Lambda^{\otimes n}
and

(15.1)

\begin{split}d_{0}(v\otimes\lambda_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda_{n})&=v\lambda_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda_{n},\\
d_{i}(v\otimes\lambda_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda_{n})&=v\otimes\lambda_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i+1}\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda_{n},\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq i\leq n-1,\\
d_{n}(v\otimes\lambda_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda_{n})&=\lambda_{n}v\otimes\lambda_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda_{n-1},\\
s_{i}(v\otimes\lambda_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda_{n})&=v\otimes\lambda_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda_{i}\otimes 1\otimes\lambda_{i+1}\dots\otimes\lambda_{n}.\end{split}

Then Hochschild homology of
\Lambda
with coefficients in
V
can be defined as

(15.2)

HH_{*}(\Lambda,V)=H_{*}(\mathsf{N}(A(\Lambda,V))).

A vector subspace
M
of
\Lambda
is called pointed if
1\in M.
A marked algebra is a couple
(\Lambda,M),
where
M
is a pointed vector subspace of
\Lambda
. For a marked algebra we define a path submodule
B(\Lambda,M,V)\subseteq A(\Lambda,V)
by the formula
B(\Lambda,M,V)=V\bar{\otimes}M^{\bar{\otimes}n}.
It is easy to check that
B(\Lambda,M,V)
is indeed a path submodule of
A(\Lambda,M).
Then we define the path
Hochschild homology of the marked algebra
(\Lambda,M)
with coefficients in
V
as the homology of the corresponding path pair of modules an set

(15.3)

\begin{split}\mathpzc{P}(\Lambda,M,V)&=(A(\Lambda,V),B(\Lambda,M,V)),\\
\Omega(\Lambda,M,V)&=\Omega(\mathpzc{P}(\Lambda,M,V)),\\
{\sf PHH}_{*}(\Lambda,M,V)&=H_{*}(\Omega(\Lambda,M,V)).\end{split}

The algebra
\Lambda
can be considered as a bimodule over itself. We set
\Omega(\Lambda,M)=\Omega(\Lambda,M,\Lambda)
and
{\sf PHH}_{*}(\Lambda,M)={\sf PHH}_{*}(\Lambda,M,\Lambda).

15.2.
Eilenberg-Zilber theorem for marked algebras

Proposition 15.1
.

Let
(\Lambda,M)
and
(\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime})

be marked algebras, and
V
and
V^{\prime}
be bimodules over
\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{\prime}
respectively. Then there is an isomorphism

(15.4)

\Omega(\Lambda\otimes\Lambda^{\prime},M\otimes\mathbb{K}+\mathbb{K}\otimes M^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime})\cong\Omega(\Lambda,M,V)\otimes\Omega(\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime},V^{\prime}).

In particular, we have

(15.5)

\Omega(\Lambda\otimes\Lambda^{\prime},M\otimes\mathbb{K}+\mathbb{K}\otimes M^{\prime})\cong\Omega(\Lambda,M)\otimes\Omega(\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime})

and

(15.6)

{\sf PHH}_{*}(\Lambda\otimes\Lambda^{\prime},M\otimes\mathbb{K}+\mathbb{K}\otimes M^{\prime})\cong{\sf PHH}_{*}(\Lambda,M)\otimes{\sf PHH}_{*}(\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime}).

Proof.

In order to use Corollary
8.10
we only need to prove that

(15.7)

\mathpzc{P}(\Lambda\otimes\Lambda^{\prime},M\otimes\mathbb{K}+\mathbb{K}\otimes M^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime})\cong\mathpzc{P}(\Lambda,M,V)\square\mathpzc{P}(\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime},V^{\prime}).

Consider the map

(15.8)

\theta:A(\Lambda,V)\otimes A(\Lambda^{\prime},V^{\prime})\longrightarrow A(\Lambda\otimes\Lambda^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime})

defined by

(15.9)

\begin{split}&\theta((v\otimes\lambda_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda_{n})\otimes(v^{\prime}\otimes\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\dots\otimes\lambda_{n}^{\prime}))=\\
&=(v\otimes v^{\prime})\otimes(\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{1}^{\prime})\otimes\dots\otimes(\lambda_{n}\otimes\lambda_{n}^{\prime}).\end{split}

Obviously,
\tau
is an isomorphism of simplicial modules. So, it is sufficient to prove that

(15.10)

\theta((B(\Lambda,M,V)\diamond B(\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime},V^{\prime}))_{n})=B(\Lambda\otimes\Lambda^{\prime},M\otimes\mathbb{K}+\mathbb{K}\otimes M^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime})_{n}

for any
n.
Set
M_{1}=M
and
M_{0}=\mathbb{K}.
For a subset
I\subseteq\{0,\dots,n-1\}
we set
M_{I}=M_{I(0)}\otimes\dots\otimes M_{I(n-1)},
where
I(x)=1,
if
x\in I,
and
I(x)=0,
if
x\notin I.

Then for any surjective order preserving map
\sigma:[n]\to[k]
we have
\sigma^{*}(V\otimes M^{\otimes k})=V\otimes M_{\mathsf{Ker}(\sigma)}.

Similarly we define
M^{\prime}_{I}
and obtain
\tau^{*}(V^{\prime}\otimes(M^{\prime})^{\otimes l})=V^{\prime}\otimes M^{\prime}_{\mathsf{Ker}(\tau)}
for any surjective order preserving map
\tau:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[l].

Therefore, it is sufficient to prove that

(15.11)

\theta\left(\sum_{I\cup J=\{0,\dots,n-1\}}(V\otimes M_{I})\otimes(V^{\prime}\otimes M^{\prime}_{J})\right)=(V\otimes V^{\prime})\otimes(M\otimes\mathbb{K}+\mathbb{K}\otimes M^{\prime})^{\otimes n}

which follows from the fact that

(15.12)

(M\otimes\mathbb{K}+\mathbb{K}\otimes M^{\prime})^{\otimes n}=\sum_{I\sqcup J=\{0,\dots,n-1\}}(M_{I(0)}\otimes M^{\prime}_{J(0)})\otimes\dots\otimes(M_{I(n-1)}\otimes M^{\prime}_{J(n-1)}).

∎

15.3.
Isomorphism lemma for marked algebras

For a vector subspace of an algebra
M\subseteq\Lambda
we denote by
M^{2}
the vector subspace generated by all pairwise products from
M.

Proposition 15.2
.

Let

f:\Lambda\to\Lambda^{\prime}
be an algebra homomorphism and
M,M^{\prime}
be pointed vector subspaces of these algebras such that
f
induces isomorphisms
M\cong M^{\prime}
and
M^{2}\cong(M^{\prime})^{2}.
Then for any
\Lambda^{\prime}
-bimodule
V
the homomorphism
f
induces an isomorphism

(15.13)

\Omega(\Lambda,M,V)\cong\Omega(\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime},V),

where
V
is considered as a
\Lambda
-bimodule via
f.

Proof.

Set
\overline{\Lambda}=\Lambda/\mathbb{K},

\overline{M}=M/\mathbb{K}
and
\overline{M^{2}}=M^{2}/\mathbb{K}.
And similarly for
\overline{\Lambda^{\prime}},\overline{M^{\prime}},\overline{(M^{\prime})^{2}}.
Then

(15.14)

\mathsf{N}A(\Lambda,V)_{n}=V\otimes\overline{\Lambda}^{\otimes n}.

Since
\mathbb{K}
is a field,
\bar{M}^{\otimes n}
is a vector subspace of
\bar{\Lambda}^{\otimes n}
and we see that

(15.15)

\overline{B(\Lambda,M,V)}=V\otimes\overline{M}^{\otimes n}.

Similar formulas hold for
\overline{B(\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime},V)},
and for
\overline{B(\Lambda,M^{2},V)}
and
\overline{B(\Lambda^{\prime},(M^{\prime})^{2},V)}.
Since
f
induces isomorphisms
\overline{M}\cong\overline{M^{\prime}}
and
\overline{M^{2}}\cong\overline{(M^{\prime})^{2}},
these formulas imply that the map
\mathsf{N}A(\Lambda,V)\to\mathsf{N}A(\Lambda^{\prime},V)
induces isomorphisms
\overline{B(\Lambda,M,V)}\cong\overline{B(\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime},V)}
and
\overline{B(\Lambda,M^{2},V)}\cong\overline{B(\Lambda^{\prime},(M^{\prime})^{2},V)}.
Then the assertion follows from Proposition
3.6
.
∎

16.
Appendix. Box product of path sets via Day convolution

The aim of this section is to present a more categorial point of view on box product of path pairs by introducing a box product of path sets. We show that the functor
\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}:\Pi^{\sf op}\times\Pi\times\Pi\to{\sf Sets}
gives rise a structure of pro-monoidal category on
\Pi,
that defines the box product on the category of path sets by the Day convolution
[
7
]
,
[
6
]
,
[
19
]
. For simplicity we will consider only path sets here, however, this can be easily generalised to path objects of a Benabou cosmos.

16.1.
Pro-functors

Here we remind the notion of a pro-functor (also called distributor). A more detailed review of this theory can be found in
[
2
, §7.8]
.

Let
\mathpzc{C}
and
\mathpzc{D}
be categories. A profuctor
\mathpzc{F}:\mathpzc{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathpzc{D}
is a functor
\mathpzc{F}:\mathpzc{D}^{\sf op}\times\mathpzc{C}\to{\sf Sets}.
The composition of two profunctors
\mathpzc{F}:\mathpzc{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathpzc{D}
and
\mathpzc{G}:\mathpzc{D}\rightsquigarrow\mathpzc{M}
is defined as the coend

(16.1)

(\mathpzc{G}\odot\mathpzc{F})(e,c)=\int^{d}\mathpzc{G}(e,d)\times\mathpzc{F}(d,c).

This composition is associative up to natural isomorphism.

Every functor
f:\mathpzc{C}\to\mathpzc{D}
defines a profunctor
\mathpzc{D}(1,f):\mathpzc{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathpzc{D}
given by
\mathpzc{D}(1,f)(d,c)=\mathpzc{D}(d,f(c)).
An advantage of profunctors over functors is that for any subcategories
\mathpzc{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathpzc{C}
and
\mathpzc{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathpzc{D}
a profunctor
\mathpzc{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathpzc{D}
induces a profunctor
\mathpzc{C}^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\mathpzc{D}^{\prime}.

For a category
\mathpzc{C}
we denote by
{\sf PSh}(\mathpzc{C})
the category of presheaves. If we denote by
\mathbf{1}
the category with one object, then a presheaf on
\mathpzc{C}
is a pro-functor
\mathbf{1}\to\mathpzc{C}.
Any pro-functor
\mathpzc{F}:\mathpzc{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathpzc{D}
defines a functor

(16.2)

\mathpzc{F}_{*}:{\sf PSh}(\mathpzc{C})\longrightarrow{\sf PSh}(\mathpzc{D})

given by the composition (
16.1
). Moreover, we have a natural isomorphism

(16.3)

\mathpzc{G}_{*}\circ\mathpzc{F}_{*}\cong(\mathpzc{G}\odot\mathpzc{F})_{*}.

16.2.
Pro-monoidal category

A pro-monoidal category is a category
\mathpzc{C}
together with the following data

•

a profunctor
\mathpzc{P}:\mathpzc{C}\times\mathpzc{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathpzc{C};

•

a profunctor
\mathpzc{J}:\mathbf{1}\rightsquigarrow\mathpzc{C};

•

associativity isomorphism
\alpha:\mathpzc{P}\odot(\mathpzc{P}\times 1)\cong\mathpzc{P}\odot(1\times\mathpzc{P})

•

unit isomorphisms
\lambda:\mathpzc{P}\odot\mathpzc{J}\cong\mathpzc{P}
and
\rho:\mathpzc{J}\odot\mathpzc{P}\cong\mathpzc{P}.

satisfying the pentagon and the unit conditions (see
[
6
, Def.2.1.1]
for details). Any monoidal category is a pro-monoidal category where
\mathpzc{P}:\mathpzc{C}^{\sf op}\times\mathpzc{C}\times\mathpzc{C}\to{\sf Sets}
is defined as

(16.4)

\mathpzc{P}(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})=\mathpzc{C}(c_{1},c_{2}\otimes c_{3})

and
\mathpzc{J}:\mathpzc{C}^{\sf op}\to{\sf Sets}
is defined as
\mathpzc{J}(c)=\mathpzc{C}(c,1{C}).

An advantage of promonoidal categories over monoidal categories is that a subcategory of a promonoidal category has an induced structure of a promonoidal category. On the other hand the category of presheaves on a promonoidal category has a natural structure of a monoidal category, which is called Day convolution.

16.3.
Day convolution

Assume that
(\mathpzc{C},\mathpzc{P},\mathpzc{J},\alpha,\rho,\lambda)
is a pro-monoidal category. Then we can define the monoidal structure on the category of presheaves
{\sf PSh}(\mathpzc{C}),
where the tensor product is defined as the coend

(16.5)

(X\otimes Y)(c)=\int^{c_{1},c_{2}}\mathpzc{P}(c,c_{1},c_{2})\times X(c_{1})\times Y(c_{2})

and
\mathpzc{J}\in{\sf PSh}(\mathpzc{C})
is the unit object.

16.4.
Box product of path sets

Consider the functor
\mathpzc{P}=\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}

(16.6)

\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}:\Pi^{\sf op}\times\Pi\times\Pi\longrightarrow{\sf Sets}

defined in (
6.6
),
and the one-point path set
\mathpzc{J}=*:\Pi^{\sf op}\to{\sf Sets},
which can be defined by the formula
\mathpzc{J}_{n}=*=\Pi([n],[0]).

We claim that they define a pro-monoidal structure on
\Pi.
Indeed, consider the embedding to the category of quivers
\mathsf{q}:\Pi\to{\sf Quiv}
(Proposition
5.1
). It is easy to check that the box-product of quivers defines a monoidal structure on
{\sf Quiv},
where the unit object is
\mathsf{q}^{0}.
Then it can be restricted to a structure of pro-monoidal category on the full subcategory
\mathsf{q}(\Pi)\subseteq{\sf Quiv}
consisted of the quivers
\mathsf{q}^{n}.
Since a subcategory of a monoidal category inherits a promonoidal structure, we obtain that
\mathsf{q}(\Pi)
is a promonoidal category, where the promonoidal structure is defined by the functors

\mathpzc{P}(\mathsf{q}^{n},\mathsf{q}^{k},\mathsf{q}^{l})={\sf Quiv}(\mathsf{q}^{n},\mathsf{q}^{k}\square\mathsf{q}^{l})
and
\mathpzc{J}(\mathsf{q}^{n})={\sf Quiv}(\mathsf{q}^{n},\mathsf{q}^{0})=*.

Since the category
\Pi
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{q}(\Pi),
the isomorphism

(16.7)

\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n;k,l)\cong{\sf Quiv}(\mathsf{q}^{n},\mathsf{q}^{k}\square\mathsf{q}^{l})

(Proposition
6.1
) implies that
\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}
defines a structure of promonoidal category on
\Pi
.

Then we can define the box product of path sets
P,P^{\prime}\in{\sf PSh}(\Pi)
as the Day convolution:

(16.8)

P\square P^{\prime}=\int^{[k],[l]}\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(-;k,l)\times P_{k}\times P^{\prime}_{l}.

Now we will define a map

(16.9)

\theta:P\square P^{\prime}\longrightarrow P\times P^{\prime}.

For any
n,k,l
there is a map

(16.10)

\theta_{n,k,l}:\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n,k,l)\times P_{k}\times P_{l}\longrightarrow P_{n}\times P^{\prime}_{n},

(16.11)

((f,g),x,y)\mapsto(f^{*}(x),g^{*}(y)).

It is easy to check that for any two morphisms
\varphi:[k]\to[k^{\prime}]
and
\psi:[l]\to[l^{\prime}]
of
\Pi
the diagram

(16.12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n,k,l)\times P_{k}\times P_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n,k,l)\times P_{k^{\prime}}\times P_{l^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n}\times P^{\prime}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n,k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\times P_{k^{\prime}}\times P_{l^{\prime}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{n,k,l}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\varphi^{*}\times\psi^{*}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi,\psi)_{*}\times 1\times 1}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{n,k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}}

Then by the universal property of coend we obtain a map (
16.9
).

Note that
{\sf Im}(\theta_{n,k,l})=\bigcup_{(f,g)\in\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n,k,l)}f^{*}(P_{k})\times g^{*}(P^{\prime}_{l}).
Therefore

(16.13)

{\sf Im}(\theta_{n}:(P\square P^{\prime})_{n}\to P_{n}\times P^{\prime}_{n})=\bigcup_{(f,g)\in\mathsf{P}\Pi_{\square}(n)}f^{*}(P_{k})\times g^{*}(P_{l}^{\prime}).

Therefore Lemma
9.1
implies that in the definition of the box product of path pairs
(X,Y)\square(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})=(X\times X^{\prime},Y\diamond Y^{\prime})
the path set
Y\diamond Y^{\prime}
is the image of the path set
Y\square Y^{\prime}:

(16.14)

Y\diamond Y^{\prime}={\sf Im}(Y\square Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow X\times X^{\prime}).
